विष्णुसहस्रनामस्तोत्रम्

Viṣṇusahasranāmastotram

Viṣṇu Sahasranāma

Mahābhārata, Anuśāsanaparvan · meanings following Śaṅkara's bhāṣya

The thousand names of Viṣṇu, taught by Bhīṣma to Yudhiṣṭhira (Mahābhārata, Anuśāsanaparvan, in the vulgate text Śaṅkara comments on): Yudhiṣṭhira's six questions and Bhīṣma's answer, the thousand names, and the fruit of reciting them. Meanings and translation follow Śaṅkara's bhāṣya.

SourceSrirangam edition, 1910
पूर्वभागः
Pūrvabhāgaḥ · The questions and the answer
श्रीवैशम्पायन उवाचśrīvaiśampāyana uvāca
श्रुत्वा धर्मानशेषेण पावनानि च सर्वशः । युधिष्ठिरः शान्तनवं पुनरेवाभ्यभाषत ॥ १ ॥
śrutvā dharmān aśeṣeṇa pāvanāni ca sarvaśaḥ | yudhiṣṭhiraḥ śāntanavaṃ punar evābhyabhāṣata || 1 ||
Padaccheda
श्रुत्वा धर्मान् अशेषेण पावनानि च सर्वशः युधिष्ठिरः शान्तनवम् पुनः एव अभ्यभाषत
śrutvā dharmān aśeṣeṇa pāvanāni ca sarvaśaḥ yudhiṣṭhiraḥ śāntanavam punaḥ eva abhyabhāṣata
Word by word
Having heard the dharmas without remainder, and the purifying (secrets of dharma) in every way, Yudhiṣṭhira spoke again to the son of Śantanu (Bhīṣma).

Introduction Introducing the stotra, opens with three verses of his own: homage to Kṛṣṇa, whose form is being, consciousness and bliss, who does his work untiringly, who is known from the Vedānta, the teacher, the witness of the intellect; to Kṛṣṇa Dvaipāyana Vyāsa, intent on the good of all beings, a sun to the lotus of the Veda, the dwelling of calm and the other virtues; and the hymn of the thousand names of Puruṣottama, of a thousand forms, with a thousand eyes, faces, feet and arms, is now explained, for the stilling of birth, old age and the rest.

Bhāṣya Vaiśampāyana says this to Janamejaya. dharmān: the dharmas known by injunction, causes of prosperity and of the highest good; aśeṣeṇa: wholly; pāvanāni: those that destroy sin, the secrets of dharma; sarvaśaḥ: in every manner. Having heard them, Yudhiṣṭhira, the son of Dharma, asked Bhīṣma, the son of Śantanu, again, thinking that a means to every human end, easy to accomplish, needing little effort and bearing great fruit, had not yet been told.

युधिष्ठिर उवाचyudhiṣṭhira uvāca
किमेकं दैवतं लोके किं वाप्येकं परायणम् । स्तुवन्तः कं कमर्चन्तः प्राप्नुयुर्मानवाः शुभम् ॥ २ ॥
kim ekaṃ daivataṃ loke kiṃ vāpy ekaṃ parāyaṇam | stuvantaḥ kaṃ kam arcantaḥ prāpnuyur mānavāḥ śubham || 2 ||
Padaccheda
किम् एकम् दैवतम् लोके किम् वा अपि एकम् परायणम् स्तुवन्तः कम् कम् अर्चन्तः प्राप्नुयुः मानवाः शुभम्
kim ekam daivatam loke kim vā api ekam parāyaṇam stuvantaḥ kam kam arcantaḥ prāpnuyuḥ mānavāḥ śubham
Word by word
What is the one deity in the world? And what is the one supreme goal? Praising whom, worshipping whom, would men gain the good?

Bhāṣya Daivatam is deva, the suffix having its own sense. loke: in the world, that is, in all the branches of learning, which are the means of making things known (lokana): what is declared there as the one deity? That is the first question. parāyaṇam: the supreme place to be reached: by whose command all act; on reaching which the knot of the heart is cut (Muṇḍaka 2.2.9); by the mere knowing of which liberation, which is bliss, is gained; knowing which one fears nothing; entering which there is no rebirth; knowing which one becomes that very thing (‘he who knows Brahman becomes Brahman’, Muṇḍaka 3.2.9); apart from which men have no other path (Śvetāśvatara 3.8): what is that one goal? That is the second question.

Bhāṣya Praising which god, telling his qualities, and worshipping which god, with worship outer or inner of many kinds, would men, the sons of Manu, gain the good, the fruit of heaven, liberation and the rest? These are two more questions (the third and the fourth).

को धर्मः सर्वधर्माणां भवतः परमो मतः । किं जपन्मुच्यते जन्तुर्जन्मसंसारबन्धनात् ॥ ३ ॥
ko dharmaḥ sarvadharmāṇāṃ bhavataḥ paramo mataḥ | kiṃ japan mucyate jantur janmasaṃsārabandhanāt || 3 ||
Padaccheda
कः धर्मः सर्व-धर्माणाम् भवतः परमः मतः किम् जपन् मुच्यते जन्तुः जन्म-संसार-बन्धनात्
kaḥ dharmaḥ sarva-dharmāṇām bhavataḥ paramaḥ mataḥ kim japan mucyate jantuḥ janma-saṃsāra-bandhanāt
Word by word
Which dharma, of all dharmas, do you hold to be the highest? Repeating what is a creature freed from the bondage of birth and the round (of birth and death)?

Bhāṣya Of all dharmas of the kind described, which is held by you to be the highest? That is the fifth question. japan: doing japa aloud, under the breath, or in the mind. jantu: one subject to birth; the word shows that all beings are entitled, as befits each, to japa, worship, praise and the rest. janma stands for all the effects that ignorance spreads out, saṃsāra for ignorance itself; freed from the bondage of these two: that is the sixth question. Being freed stands for the other fruits too; liberation is named to show that it is the chief.

Bhāṣya Six questions have been asked. Of them the sixth, the last, which is about what is to be repeated, is answered by the next verse.

श्रीभीष्म उवाचśrībhīṣma uvāca
जगत्प्रभुं देवदेवमनन्तं पुरुषोत्तमम् । स्तुवन्नामसहस्रेण पुरुषः सततोत्थितः ॥ ४ ॥
jagatprabhuṃ devadevam anantaṃ puruṣottamam | stuvan nāmasahasreṇa puruṣaḥ satatotthitaḥ || 4 ||
Padaccheda
जगत्-प्रभुम् देव-देवम् अनन्तम् पुरुषोत्तमम् स्तुवन् नाम-सहस्रेण पुरुषः सतत-उत्थितः
jagat-prabhum deva-devam anantam puruṣottamam stuvan nāma-sahasreṇa puruṣaḥ satata-utthitaḥ
Word by word
A man who, ever diligent, praises with the thousand names the lord of the world, the god of gods, the infinite, Puruṣottama, (goes beyond all sorrows).

Bhāṣya Bhīṣma, the cause of fear to all enemies outer and inner, the teacher of the Mokṣadharma and the rest, all-knowing, speaks. jagatprabhu: the master of the world, moving and unmoving. devadeva: the god of the gods, Brahmā and the others. ananta: not bounded by place, time or thing. puruṣottama: higher than the perishable and the imperishable, effect and cause. nāmasahasreṇa: with the thousand names, telling his qualities. satatotthitaḥ: constantly intent. puruṣa: so called from lying in the city (of the body), or from being full. ‘Goes beyond all sorrows’ (verse 6) is to be joined with each.

तमेव चार्चयन्नित्यं भक्त्या पुरुषमव्ययम् । ध्यायन्स्तुवन्नमस्यंश्च यजमानस्तमेव च ॥ ५ ॥
tam eva cārcayan nityaṃ bhaktyā puruṣam avyayam | dhyāyan stuvan namasyaṃś ca yajamānas tam eva ca || 5 ||
Padaccheda
तम् एव च अर्चयन् नित्यम् भक्त्या पुरुषम् अव्ययम् ध्यायन् स्तुवन् नमस्यन् च यजमानः तम् एव च
tam eva ca arcayan nityam bhaktyā puruṣam avyayam dhyāyan stuvan namasyan ca yajamānaḥ tam eva ca
Word by word
And the worshipper who always worships with devotion that same undecaying Puruṣa, meditating on him, praising him and bowing to him, (goes beyond all sorrows).

Bhāṣya This answers the fourth question. arcayan: doing outer worship, at all times; bhakti: devotion, being wholly intent on him; avyaya: free of destruction and change; dhyāyan: doing inner worship; stuvan and namasyan: praising him and bowing to him with the same devotion, praise and bowing being both parts of worship. yajamāna: the worshipper, who enjoys the fruit. Or arcayan names both kinds of worship, and meditating, praising and bowing are its mental, verbal and bodily forms.

अनादिनिधनं विष्णुं सर्वलोकमहेश्वरम् । लोकाध्यक्षं स्तुवन्नित्यं सर्वदुःखातिगो भवेत् ॥ ६ ॥
anādinidhanaṃ viṣṇuṃ sarvalokamaheśvaram | lokādhyakṣaṃ stuvan nityaṃ sarvaduḥkhātigo bhavet || 6 ||
Padaccheda
अनादि-निधनम् विष्णुम् सर्व-लोक-महेश्वरम् लोक-अध्यक्षम् स्तुवन् नित्यम् सर्व-दुःख-अतिगः भवेत्
anādi-nidhanam viṣṇum sarva-loka-maheśvaram loka-adhyakṣam stuvan nityam sarva-duḥkha-atigaḥ bhavet
Word by word
Praising always Viṣṇu, who has no beginning or end, the great lord of all the worlds, the overseer of the world, one goes beyond all sorrows.

Bhāṣya The third question is answered by these three quarters. anādinidhana: free of the six changes of being. viṣṇu: the pervader. sarvalokamaheśvara: loka is what is seen, the visible world; he is lord even of Brahmā and the others who rule it. lokādhyakṣa: who sees the world directly by his own natural knowing. Praising him constantly, one goes beyond all sorrows, those of the body and the rest: this fruit is common to the three (verses 4–6).

Note The edition prints no number with this verse; it is the sixth.

ब्रह्मण्यं सर्वधर्मज्ञं लोकानां कीर्तिवर्धनम् । लोकनाथं महद्भूतं सर्वभूतभवोद्भवम् ॥ ७ ॥
brahmaṇyaṃ sarvadharmajñaṃ lokānāṃ kīrtivardhanam | lokanāthaṃ mahad bhūtaṃ sarvabhūtabhavodbhavam || 7 ||
Padaccheda
ब्रह्मण्यम् सर्व-धर्म-ज्ञम् लोकानाम् कीर्ति-वर्धनम् लोक-नाथम् महत् भूतम् सर्व-भूत-भव-उद्भवम्
brahmaṇyam sarva-dharma-jñam lokānām kīrti-vardhanam loka-nātham mahat bhūtam sarva-bhūta-bhava-udbhavam
Word by word
(Praising him) who is devoted to brahman, who knows all dharmas, who increases the fame of the worlds, the lord of the worlds, the great being, from whom the becoming of all beings arises.

Bhāṣya He further describes the one to be praised. brahmaṇya: good to brahman: to creation, to the brāhmaṇa, to austerity, or to the Veda. sarvadharmajña: who knows all dharmas. kīrtivardhana: who increases the fame of beings by entering them with his own power. lokanātha: he to whom the worlds pray, or who afflicts (upatapati), blesses or rules the worlds (the senses of nāth). mahad bhūtam: Brahman, great because it surpasses all, and being, the truth in the highest sense. sarvabhūtabhavodbhava: from whom arises the becoming, the round of birth and death, of all beings.

एष मे सर्वधर्माणां धर्मोऽधिकतमो मतः । यद्भक्त्या पुण्डरीकाक्षं स्तवैरर्चेन्नरः सदा ॥ ८ ॥
eṣa me sarvadharmāṇāṃ dharmo 'dhikatamo mataḥ | yad bhaktyā puṇḍarīkākṣaṃ stavair arcen naraḥ sadā || 8 ||
Padaccheda
एषः मे सर्व-धर्माणाम् धर्मः अधिकतमः मतः यत् भक्त्या पुण्डरीक-अक्षम् स्तवैः अर्चेत् नरः सदा
eṣaḥ me sarva-dharmāṇām dharmaḥ adhikatamaḥ mataḥ yat bhaktyā puṇḍarīka-akṣam stavaiḥ arcet naraḥ sadā
Word by word
Of all dharmas this is held by me to be the highest dharma: that a man should always worship the lotus-eyed with devotion, by hymns.

Bhāṣya This answers the fifth question. Of all the dharmas known by injunction, this, about to be told, is in my view the highest: that a man always, with devotion, worships with hymns, praises that tell his qualities, the lotus-eyed, Vāsudeva, who shines in the lotus of the heart.

Bhāṣya On why it is the highest: it needs no injury, no other person, no other material, no rule of place and time. So the Viṣṇu Purāṇa: ‘what one gains by meditation in the Kṛta age, by sacrifices in the Tretā, by worship in the Dvāpara, one gains in the Kali by praising Keśava’; Manu: ‘by japa alone a brāhmaṇa is fulfilled, there is no doubt; whether he does anything else or not, the friend (of all) is called a brāhmaṇa’; the Mahābhārata: ‘japa is called the highest dharma of all; the sacrifice of japa goes on without harm to beings’; and the Lord: ‘of sacrifices I am the sacrifice of japa’ (Gītā 10.25).

परमं यो महत्तेजः परमं यो महत्तपः । परमं यो महद्ब्रह्म परमं यः परायणम् ॥ ९ ॥
paramaṃ yo mahat tejaḥ paramaṃ yo mahat tapaḥ | paramaṃ yo mahad brahma paramaṃ yaḥ parāyaṇam || 9 ||
Padaccheda
परमम् यः महत् तेजः परमम् यः महत् तपः परमम् यः महत् ब्रह्म परमम् यः परायणम्
paramam yaḥ mahat tejaḥ paramam yaḥ mahat tapaḥ paramam yaḥ mahat brahma paramam yaḥ parāyaṇam
Word by word
He who is the supreme, great light; he who is the supreme, great ruling power; he who is the supreme, great Brahman; he who is the supreme goal: (he is the one goal of all beings).

Bhāṣya This settles the second question. tejas: the light that is consciousness, revealing all (‘by whose light the sun, kindled, burns’; ‘the gods worship him, the light of lights’; ‘there the sun does not shine’, Muṇḍaka 2.2.11; ‘the light that is in the sun’, Gītā 15.12). tapas: he who commands (tapati); his ruling of all is taught in the Antaryāmi-brāhmaṇa (‘who, being within, rules this world, the other world and all beings’, Bṛhadāraṇyaka 3.7.1) and in the Taittirīya (‘from fear of him the wind blows’, 2.8); or tapati means ‘rules’ (īṣṭe); great, because his lordship is unbounded (‘he is the lord of all’). brahman: the supreme Brahman, truth and the rest, great as worthy of worship. parāyaṇa: the supreme goal, free of any doubt of return.

Bhāṣya ‘supreme’ excludes the lower lights, the sun and the like. ‘Who’ is to be read each time as ‘the god who’: the god who is the supreme light, the supreme ruling power, the supreme Brahman, the supreme goal, he is the one goal of all beings: that is the sense of the sentence.

पवित्राणां पवित्रं यो मङ्गलानां च मङ्गलम् । दैवतं देवतानां च भूतानां योऽव्ययः पिता ॥ १० ॥
pavitrāṇāṃ pavitraṃ yo maṅgalānāṃ ca maṅgalam | daivataṃ devatānāṃ ca bhūtānāṃ yo 'vyayaḥ pitā || 10 ||
Padaccheda
पवित्राणाम् पवित्रम् यः मङ्गलानाम् च मङ्गलम् दैवतम् देवतानाम् च भूतानाम् यः अव्ययः पिता
pavitrāṇām pavitram yaḥ maṅgalānām ca maṅgalam daivatam devatānām ca bhūtānām yaḥ avyayaḥ pitā
Word by word
He who is the purifier of purifiers, the auspicious of auspicious things, the deity of deities, the undecaying father of beings: (he is the one deity in the world).

Bhāṣya This answers the first question. pavitrāṇāṃ pavitram: the purifier of the things that purify, holy places and the rest; for the supreme Puruṣa, meditated on, seen, named, praised, worshipped, remembered and bowed to, roots out all sins; or he destroys, by the knowledge of his true nature, the action of merit and demerit that causes the bondage of the round, and ignorance, its cause. He supports this with many verses of the Purāṇas and Itihāsas on the power of meditating on Hari and naming him (‘naming Keśava in the Kali age’; ‘as fire to metals, so the naming of him with devotion to all sins’; ‘even one bow to Kṛṣṇa equals ten horse sacrifices; the one who sacrificed returns to birth, the one who bowed to Kṛṣṇa does not’, and others). maṅgalānāṃ maṅgalam: maṅgala is happiness, its means and what makes it known; he is the supreme good, whose nature is the highest bliss. daivataṃ devatānām: the god of gods, surpassing them in shining and the rest. bhūtānāṃ avyayaḥ pitā: the undecaying father of beings. The god who is these is the one deity in the world: that is the sense of the sentence.

Bhāṣya On there being one god: ‘one god, hidden in all beings, all-pervading, the inner self of all beings’ (Śvetāśvatara 6.11); ‘one only, without a second’ (Chāndogya 6.2.1). If it is said that there cannot be one god since the jīva and the supreme differ: no, scripture teaches that the supreme, unchanged, entered as the witness of the intellect and its states (‘having created it, he entered it’, Taittirīya 2.6; ‘he has entered here, to the tips of the nails’, Bṛhadāraṇyaka 1.4.7). If it is said that those who entered differ among themselves: no, it is the one who is heard of as entering in many ways (‘one god, settled in many ways’; ‘as the one fire, entering the world, takes the form of each thing’, Kaṭha 2.2.9–10; ‘one truth the sages call by many names’, Ṛgveda 1.164.46). So the Upaniṣads, the Gītā (‘who worships me as dwelling in all beings, resting in oneness’, 6.31; ‘I am the Self seated in the heart of all beings’, 10.20), the Harivaṃśa, Manu, and the Viṣṇu Purāṇa (‘I, you and all this are the Self; give up the delusion of difference’) and other Purāṇas, where Maheśvara says that those who see him as other than Hari sin, and that there is no difference between Hari and himself.

Bhāṣya On the same: ‘they acknowledge him as the Self and make it known’ (Brahmasūtra 4.1.3). The Jābālas take him as the Self, ‘I am you, O god, and you are I’; and the Vedānta teaches him as the Self (‘he is your Self, the inner controller, the immortal’; ‘that is the Self; that you are’). This is not a symbol-view, like seeing Viṣṇu in an image: that would make the teaching figurative, and in a symbol-view the statement is made once, ‘the mind is Brahman’, whereas here it is made both ways, ‘you are I, I am you’. And seeing difference is condemned (‘he who worships another deity, thinking “he is one, I am another”, does not know’, Bṛhadāraṇyaka 1.4.10; ‘he goes from death to death who sees as if there were difference here’, Kaṭha 2.1.10), while seeing oneness is taught as right knowledge (Gītā 4.35, 18.20). Even on the view that ignorance is the limiting adjunct, scripture and tradition bear it out: the difference of Self and Brahman is made by ignorance and ends with it, as the many spaces of pots are one space; ‘the manifold is mere mind; non-duality is the highest truth’ (Gauḍapāda).

Bhāṣya On the authority of the Veda: the Veda is a valid source also for what already exists, the Self, and not only for what is to be done; words convey meanings related to one another, not only to something to be done, or the arthavāda passages would not connect; it is desire that moves one to act, not the injunction (‘as is his desire, so is his will; as his will, so his deed’, Bṛhadāraṇyaka 4.4.5; Manu 2.4). Mantras and arthavādas are valid even where their aim is something else: Nahuṣa, who denied the authority of the mantras for the sprinkling of cows and kicked Agastya, became a serpent (Mahābhārata). So the knowledge of the Self is to be received with faith (Gītā 9.3; the Aitareya: ‘this is the path, this the work, this Brahman, this the truth; let one not neglect it’, with the ṛc ‘three races went beyond’, those who went beyond becoming trees, plants and creeping things; ‘those who slay the Self go to the sunless worlds’, Īśa 3; ‘he who knows Brahman as non-existent becomes non-existent’, Taittirīya 2.6; ‘what sin has he not done, the thief who steals the Self, who takes the Self to be other than it is?’, the story of Śakuntalā).

Bhāṣya On bathing fit for the japa of the thousand names: the bath in the tīrtha of the mind, in which the gods, the Vedas and all that purifies become one; the lake of knowledge with the water of meditation, which washes away the dirt of attachment and aversion (Mahābhārata); ‘the bath of the mind is the thinking of Viṣṇu’ (Viṣṇusmṛti); ‘pure or impure, in whatever state, he who remembers the lotus-eyed is pure within and without’.

Note The edition prints no number with this verse; it is the tenth.

यतः सर्वाणि भूतानि भवन्त्यादियुगागमे । यस्मिंश्च प्रलयं यान्ति पुनरेव युगक्षये ॥ ११ ॥
yataḥ sarvāṇi bhūtāni bhavanty ādiyugāgame | yasmiṃś ca pralayaṃ yānti punar eva yugakṣaye || 11 ||
Padaccheda
यतः सर्वाणि भूतानि भवन्ति आदि-युग-आगमे यस्मिन् च प्रलयम् यान्ति पुनः एव युग-क्षये
yataḥ sarvāṇi bhūtāni bhavanti ādi-yuga-āgame yasmin ca pralayam yānti punaḥ eva yuga-kṣaye
Word by word
From whom all beings come into being at the coming of the first age, and in whom alone they go again to dissolution at the end of the age;

Bhāṣya The one deity just set forth is now marked out. yugakṣaye: at the great dissolution. eva: in him alone, in no other. ca: and in whom they stand in between (‘that from which these beings are born’, Taittirīya 3.1).

तस्य लोकप्रधानस्य जगन्नाथस्य भूपते । विष्णोर्नामसहस्रं मे शृणु पापभयापहम् ॥ १२ ॥
tasya lokapradhānasya jagannāthasya bhūpate | viṣṇor nāmasahasraṃ me śṛṇu pāpabhayāpaham || 12 ||
Padaccheda
तस्य लोक-प्रधानस्य जगत्-नाथस्य भूपते विष्णोः नाम-सहस्रम् मे शृणु पाप-भय-अपहम्
tasya loka-pradhānasya jagat-nāthasya bhūpate viṣṇoḥ nāma-sahasram me śṛṇu pāpa-bhaya-apaham
Word by word
Hear from me, O king, the thousand names of that Viṣṇu, the chief of the worlds, the lord of the world, which take away sin and fear.

Bhāṣya Of the one deity so marked; lokapradhāna: set forth as chief by the branches of learning, the means of making the world known; jagannātha: the master of the worlds, the supreme Self, who wields māyā and yet is untouched; bhūpate: O king; viṣṇu: whose nature is to pervade. The thousand names destroy sin and fear; hear them from me with one-pointed mind, and hold them. The Viṣṇudharma says that the names have different powers, and different fruits are gained by uttering them.

Bhāṣya On how words name him: in the supreme Brahman there are no relation, quality, action, genus or convention, the grounds on which words apply; yet in Brahman with qualities and with modifications, being the Self of all, these grounds are found, so all words apply to the supreme Puruṣa.

यानि नामानि गौणानि विख्यातानि महात्मनः । ऋषिभिः परिगीतानि तानि वक्ष्यामि भूतये ॥ १३ ॥
yāni nāmāni gauṇāni vikhyātāni mahātmanaḥ | ṛṣibhiḥ parigītāni tāni vakṣyāmi bhūtaye || 13 ||
Padaccheda
यानि नामानि गौणानि विख्यातानि महात्मनः ऋषिभिः परिगीतानि तानि वक्ष्यामि भूतये
yāni nāmāni gauṇāni vikhyātāni mahātmanaḥ ṛṣibhiḥ parigītāni tāni vakṣyāmi bhūtaye
Word by word
The names of the great Self that are drawn from his qualities, well known, sung everywhere by the seers: those I shall tell, for well-being (the four human ends).

Bhāṣya Gauṇāni: arising from his qualities, applied because of qualities; among them those that are well known, sung everywhere by the seers, seers of mantras and those who know them, in the accounts of the supreme Lord. mahātman: great and the Self; ‘because it obtains, takes, eats the objects here, and because its being is unbroken, it is called ātman’; he alone is the great Self, of unthinkable power. bhūtaye: for the gaining of the four human ends, for those who seek them.

Bhāṣya Before the names: words like āditya, whose usual sense is another, still praise him in the thousand names, since those things are his glories and not other than he (‘the elements, the senses, Prakṛti, the self and the supreme Self: you alone stand fivefold’, Viṣṇu Purāṇa; Gītā 10.21–42; ‘Puruṣa alone is all this’, Muṇḍaka 2.1.10). A name that comes again, like viṣṇu, is no repetition, for it is taken in another sense by another derivation; names like śrīpati and mādhava, alike in sense, differ in word; and even sameness of sense is no fault, since the question was about one deity. Where a word is masculine, ‘Viṣṇu’ is understood; where feminine, ‘the deity’; where neuter, ‘Brahman’.

विश्वं विष्णुर्वषट्कारो भूतभव्यभवत्प्रभुः । भूतकृद्भूतभृद्भावो भूतात्मा भूतभावनः ॥ १४ ॥
viśvaṃ viṣṇur vaṣaṭkāro bhūtabhavyabhavatprabhuḥ | bhūtakṛd bhūtabhṛd bhāvo bhūtātmā bhūtabhāvanaḥ || 14 ||
Padaccheda
विश्वम् विष्णुः वषट्कारः भूत-भव्य-भवत्-प्रभुः भूत-कृत् भूत-भृत् भावः भूत-आत्मा भूत-भावनः
viśvam viṣṇuḥ vaṣaṭkāraḥ bhūta-bhavya-bhavat-prabhuḥ bhūta-kṛt bhūta-bhṛt bhāvaḥ bhūta-ātmā bhūta-bhāvanaḥ
Word by word
(He is) Viśva, the All (Brahman, the cause of all); Viṣṇu, the Pervader; Vaṣaṭkāra, he for whom ‘vaṣaṭ’ is called; the Lord of past, future and present; Bhūtakṛt, the Maker (and the Cutter) of beings; Bhūtabhṛt, the Bearer of beings; Bhāva, Being; Bhūtātmā, the Self of beings; Bhūtabhāvana, he who brings forth beings.

Bhāṣya Viśvam: Brahman is called ‘the All’ as the cause of the universe; the very first name names the cause by a word for the effect, to show that he is fitly praised even by names of effects such as Viriñci. Or: the universe is not other than the supreme Puruṣa in truth, so Brahman is called the All (‘Puruṣa alone is all this’, Muṇḍaka 2.1.10; ‘Brahman alone is all this’); nothing apart from it truly is. Or it is viśva because it enters (viśati) (‘having created it, he entered it’, Taittirīya 2.6); or because at dissolution all beings enter into it (‘into which they go and enter’, Taittirīya 3.1): either way Brahman is viśva.

Bhāṣya On the same: or the word names the syllable Om, since name and named are not wholly different: the All is Om, which is Brahman. He cites the Upaniṣads on Om (Kaṭha 1.2.15–16, Praśna 5.2 and 5.5, Taittirīya 1.8, Chāndogya 2.23.3, and Gauḍapāda's verses ‘Om is the lower and the higher Brahman … he who knows Om is a sage’) and the law books (Gītā 8.13, 10.25; ‘the Vedas begin with Om and rest in Om; all speech is Om, so let one practise Om’).

Bhāṣya On the same: ‘all this is Brahman, born from it, dissolving into it, breathing in it: so let one meditate, calm’ (Chāndogya 3.14.1): when all is one's Self, attachment and the rest cannot arise. To show that praise and bowing are to be done by one free of injury and the like, he cites the verses on seeing all as oneself (Gītā 6.32; Prahlāda's words in the Viṣṇu Purāṇa, ‘this whole world is the spread of Viṣṇu; it is to be seen as oneself, without difference’; the marks of the devotee of Viṣṇu: he does not swerve from his own dharma, is the same to friend and foe, takes nothing, harms none). They are to be done with faith, not without it (Gītā 17.28); ‘Om tat sat’ (Gītā 17.23) makes even a defective act whole; and having meditated on oneself as Viṣṇu (‘only one who is Viṣṇu should praise Viṣṇu’, Mahābhārata). One should not stay where Vāsudeva is reviled, and should have the highest devotion to God and the teacher (Śvetāśvatara 6.23).

Bhāṣya On the same: naming him destroys all sin even without faith and devotion (‘naming Vāsudeva, knowingly or unknowingly, all sin dissolves like salt in water’, and other verses); how much more the naming of the thousand names with faith. Remembering and meditating are included in naming, for ‘what one thinks with the mind, that one speaks’. The Brahman taught in answer to the six questions (verses 2–11) is what is named by ‘the All’.

Bhāṣya Viṣṇuḥ: what is that All? Viṣṇu. The Ṛgveda (‘knowing his name, proclaim it … O Viṣṇu, we seek your favour’) enjoins naming Viṣṇu for gaining right knowledge. viṣṇu: he pervades (veveṣṭi), from viṣ ‘to pervade’ with the suffix nu: free of the limits of place, time and thing (‘pervading all within and without, Nārāyaṇa stands’). Or from viś ‘to enter’ (‘because all this is entered by his power, he is called Viṣṇu’, Viṣṇu Purāṇa; ‘my splendour fills heaven and earth … by my stride I am named Viṣṇu’, Mahābhārata).

Bhāṣya Vaṣaṭkāraḥ: he for whom ‘vaṣaṭ’ is called in the sacrifice; or the sacrifice in which it is called (‘Viṣṇu is the sacrifice’); or he who pleases the gods as the mantras with vaṣaṭ; or the deity (‘Prajāpati and Vaṣaṭkāra’). bhūtabhavyabhavatprabhuḥ: lord of what was, will be and is; his lordship, beyond the divisions of time, is pure being. bhūtakṛt: as Viriñci, resting on rajas, he makes beings; as Rudra, resting on tamas, he cuts (kṛntati) them. bhūtabhṛt: resting on sattva, he bears, protects, upholds or nourishes beings. bhāvaḥ: he becomes (bhavati) in the form of the manifold, or simply is; or he is being, existence. bhūtātmā: the self of beings, the inner controller (‘he is your Self, the inner controller, the immortal’, Bṛhadāraṇyaka 3.7.3). bhūtabhāvanaḥ: who brings forth beings or makes them grow.

पूतात्मा परमात्मा च मुक्तानां परमा गतिः । अव्ययः पुरुषः साक्षी क्षेत्रज्ञोऽक्षर एव च ॥ १५ ॥
pūtātmā paramātmā ca muktānāṃ paramā gatiḥ | avyayaḥ puruṣaḥ sākṣī kṣetrajño 'kṣara eva ca || 15 ||
Padaccheda
पूत-आत्मा परम-आत्मा च मुक्तानाम् परमा गतिः अव्ययः पुरुषः साक्षी क्षेत्र-ज्ञः अक्षरः एव च
pūta-ātmā parama-ātmā ca muktānām paramā gatiḥ avyayaḥ puruṣaḥ sākṣī kṣetra-jñaḥ akṣaraḥ eva ca
Word by word
(He is) Pūtātmā, whose Self is pure; Paramātmā, the supreme Self; the highest goal of the freed; Avyaya, the Undecaying; Puruṣa; Sākṣī, the Witness; Kṣetrajña, the Knower of the field; and Akṣara, the Imperishable, (one with the knower of the field).

Introduction Names such as bhūtakṛt might suggest that he depends on the guṇas; that is now denied.

Bhāṣya Pūtātmā: he whose self is pure (‘alone and without qualities’, Śvetāśvatara 6.11); his taking on the colour of the guṇas is by his own will. paramātmā: supreme and the Self, unlike effect and cause, ever pure, conscious and free by nature. muktānāṃ paramā gatiḥ: the deity to be reached by the freed, the highest, for one who has reached him does not return (‘having come to me, there is no rebirth’, Gītā 8.16). avyayaḥ: without decay, destruction or change (‘ageless, deathless, undecaying’). puruṣaḥ: he lies (śete) in the city (pura), the body (‘pervading the holy city of nine gates, the great Self is called puruṣa’, Mahābhārata); or he was (āsīt) before (purā) (‘I was here before’, Bṛhadāraṇyaka 1.4.1); or he sits among the exalted (puruṣu), or gives many (puru) fruits, or at dissolution brings the many worlds to an end; or from filling (pūraṇa) and abiding (sadana) (Mahābhārata).

Bhāṣya Sākṣī: he sees directly, without anything between, by his own nature as knowing (Pāṇini 5.2.91). kṣetrajñaḥ: he knows the field, the body (‘know me as the knower of the field’, Gītā 13.2; Brahma Purāṇa). akṣaraḥ: he does not perish (na kṣarati), the supreme Self; or from aś ‘to pervade’. eva: the knower of the field and the imperishable are in truth not different (‘that you are’); ca: and there is a difference for practical purposes only, for common acceptance is no valid means of knowing (prasiddher apramāṇatvāt).

योगो योगविदां नेता प्रधानपुरुषेश्वरः । नारसिंहवपुः श्रीमान्केशवः पुरुषोत्तमः ॥ १६ ॥
yogo yogavidāṃ netā pradhānapuruṣeśvaraḥ | nārasiṃhavapuḥ śrīmān keśavaḥ puruṣottamaḥ || 16 ||
Padaccheda
योगः योग-विदाम् नेता प्रधान-पुरुष-ईश्वरः नारसिंह-वपुः श्रीमान् केशवः पुरुष-उत्तमः
yogaḥ yoga-vidām netā pradhāna-puruṣa-īśvaraḥ nārasiṃha-vapuḥ śrīmān keśavaḥ puruṣa-uttamaḥ
Word by word
(He is) Yoga; the Leader of those who know yoga; the Lord of Pradhāna and of Puruṣa (the jīva); he whose body is of man and lion; Śrīmān, with whom Śrī dwells; Keśava; Puruṣottama.

Bhāṣya Yogaḥ: ‘holding all the senses of knowledge with the mind, the sense of oneness of the knower of the field and the supreme Self is yoga’; since he is to be reached by it, he is called yoga. yogavidāṃ netā: the leader of those who know, reflect on or gain yoga, bearing their gain and safekeeping (Gītā 9.22). pradhānapuruṣeśvaraḥ: lord of pradhāna, the Prakṛti made of māyā, and of puruṣa, the jīva. nārasiṃhavapuḥ: whose body shows the limbs of a man and a lion. śrīmān: on whose breast Śrī always dwells.

Bhāṣya Keśavaḥ: whose hair (keśa) is beautiful, the suffix va in praise (Pāṇini 5.2.109); or ka (Brahmā), a (Viṣṇu) and īśa (Śiva), the three forms, are called keśāḥ, and stand in his control; or from slaying Keśin (‘because you have slain the wicked Keśin, you will be known by the name Keśava’, Nārada to Kṛṣṇa, Viṣṇu Purāṇa). puruṣottamaḥ: the highest of puruṣas; the compound in the genitive is allowed here, since he is not singled out from a class by genus, quality or action; or it is a compound in the ablative: ‘since I am beyond the perishable and higher than the imperishable, I am known as Puruṣottama’ (Gītā 15.18).

सर्वः शर्वः शिवः स्थाणुर्भूतादिर्निधिरव्ययः । संभवो भावनो भर्ता प्रभवः प्रभुरीश्वरः ॥ १७ ॥
sarvaḥ śarvaḥ śivaḥ sthāṇur bhūtādir nidhir avyayaḥ | saṃbhavo bhāvano bhartā prabhavaḥ prabhur īśvaraḥ || 17 ||
Padaccheda
सर्वः शर्वः शिवः स्थाणुः भूत-आदिः निधिः अव्ययः संभवः भावनः भर्ता प्रभवः प्रभुः ईश्वरः
sarvaḥ śarvaḥ śivaḥ sthāṇuḥ bhūta-ādiḥ nidhiḥ avyayaḥ saṃbhavaḥ bhāvanaḥ bhartā prabhavaḥ prabhuḥ īśvaraḥ
Word by word
(He is) Sarva, the All; Śarva, the Destroyer; Śiva, the Pure; Sthāṇu, the Steadfast; Bhūtādi, the first cause of beings; the imperishable Treasure; Saṃbhava, who comes into being (of his own will); Bhāvana, who brings forth (the fruits of action); Bhartā, the Supporter; Prabhava, the Origin; Prabhu, the Mighty; Īśvara, the Lord.

Bhāṣya Sarvaḥ: ‘because he is the origin and end of all, the existent and the non-existent, and knows all at all times, he is called Sarva’ (Vyāsa, Mahābhārata). śarvaḥ: he destroys (śṛṇāti) all creatures at dissolution. śivaḥ: pure, being free of the three guṇas; since scripture teaches that they are one (‘he is Brahmā, he is Śiva’, Kaivalya 8), it is Hari who is praised by names such as Śiva. sthāṇuḥ: because he is steady. bhūtādiḥ: the first cause of beings. nidhir avyayaḥ: the treasure in which all is laid down at dissolution (Pāṇini 3.3.93), qualified as imperishable.

Bhāṣya Saṃbhavaḥ: his coming into being is right, by his own will (‘to establish dharma I come into being age after age’, Gītā 4.8; ‘by my own will I am born, free of the sorrow of the womb’). bhāvanaḥ: he brings forth the fruits of all who enjoy them (Brahmasūtra 3.2.38). bhartā: he bears the world as its ground. prabhavaḥ: from him the great elements come forth; or his birth is eminent. prabhuḥ: most able in all acts. īśvaraḥ: his lordship has no condition (‘he is the lord of all’, Bṛhadāraṇyaka 4.4.22).

स्वयंभूः शंभुरादित्यः पुष्कराक्षो महास्वनः । अनादिनिधनो धाता विधाता धातुरुत्तमः ॥ १८ ॥
svayaṃbhūḥ śaṃbhur ādityaḥ puṣkarākṣo mahāsvanaḥ | anādinidhano dhātā vidhātā dhātur uttamaḥ || 18 ||
Padaccheda
स्वयंभूः शंभुः आदित्यः पुष्कर-अक्षः महा-स्वनः अनादि-निधनः धाता विधाता धातुः उत्तमः
svayaṃbhūḥ śaṃbhuḥ ādityaḥ puṣkara-akṣaḥ mahā-svanaḥ anādi-nidhanaḥ dhātā vidhātā dhātuḥ uttamaḥ
Word by word
(He is) Svayaṃbhū, who is of himself; Śaṃbhu, who brings happiness (to his devotees); Āditya, (the golden Puruṣa in the sun); Puṣkarākṣa, the Lotus-eyed; Mahāsvana, whose great voice (is the Veda); without beginning or end; Dhātā, the Supporter; Vidhātā, the Ordainer (of actions and their fruits); the highest Support.

Bhāṣya Svayaṃbhūḥ: he is of himself (‘he himself shone forth’, Manu 1.7); or he is above all and is by himself; or he is himself both what is above and what is below (‘encompassing, self-existent’); or, as the supreme Lord, he is independent, not dependent on another (‘the self-existent pierced the openings outward’, Kaṭha 2.1.1). śaṃbhuḥ: he brings happiness to his devotees. ādityaḥ: the golden Puruṣa within the sun's orb; or Viṣṇu among the twelve Ādityas (‘of the Ādityas I am Viṣṇu’, Gītā 10.21); or the lord of Aditi, the unbroken earth; or like the sun: as the one sun appears many in many vessels of water, so the one Self appears many in many bodies.

Bhāṣya Puṣkarākṣaḥ: whose eyes are likened to the lotus. mahāsvanaḥ: whose great, revered sound is the Veda (‘the Ṛgveda, the Yajurveda … are the breath of this great being’, Bṛhadāraṇyaka 2.4.10). anādinidhanaḥ: without birth or death. dhātā: he bears the world as Ananta and in other forms. vidhātā: the maker of actions and their fruits; or he bears in a special way even Ananta and the rest. dhātur uttamaḥ: one name, with its qualifier, ‘the highest support’; or ‘higher than Dhātṛ, Viriñci’; or two names: the conscious support, higher than all the elements, earth and the rest, since consciousness upholds the whole manifold of effect and cause; and uttama, the most exalted of the exalted.

अप्रमेयो हृषीकेशः पद्मनाभोऽमरप्रभुः । विश्वकर्मा मनुस्त्वष्टा स्थविष्ठः स्थविरो ध्रुवः ॥ १९ ॥
aprameyo hṛṣīkeśaḥ padmanābho 'maraprabhuḥ | viśvakarmā manus tvaṣṭā sthaviṣṭhaḥ sthaviro dhruvaḥ || 19 ||
Padaccheda
अप्रमेयः हृषीक-ईशः पद्म-नाभः अमर-प्रभुः विश्व-कर्मा मनुः त्वष्टा स्थविष्ठः स्थविरः ध्रुवः
aprameyaḥ hṛṣīka-īśaḥ padma-nābhaḥ amara-prabhuḥ viśva-karmā manuḥ tvaṣṭā sthaviṣṭhaḥ sthaviraḥ dhruvaḥ
Word by word
(He is) Aprameya, beyond the means of knowledge; Hṛṣīkeśa, the Lord of the senses; Padmanābha, from whose navel (the lotus of the world springs); the Lord of the immortals; Viśvakarmā, whose work is the universe; Manu, the Thinker; Tvaṣṭā, who makes (beings) thin (at dissolution); Sthaviṣṭha, the grossest; the ancient, the firm.

Bhāṣya Aprameyaḥ: not known by perception, being without sound and the other qualities; nor by inference, having no mark that goes with it; nor by comparison, being partless and so without likeness; nor by presumption, since nothing is unexplained without it; nor by non-apprehension, being positive and the witness even of absence; nor even by scripture as a means producing something new in it. How then is it known from scripture? As the witness of the means of knowledge, light by nature, it is not their object; scripture is its means only in removing what is superimposed on it. So he is immeasurable, being the witness.

Bhāṣya Hṛṣīkeśaḥ: the lord of the senses, as the knower of the field; or the supreme Self, under whose control the senses are; or he whose hair (keśa), the rays of the sun and moon, delights (hṛṣ) the world (‘the sun and moon wake and lull the world with their rays, called hair’, Mokṣadharma). padmanābhaḥ: in whose navel is the lotus, the cause of the whole world (‘in the navel of the unborn one was set the one’). amaraprabhuḥ: lord of the immortals. viśvakarmā: whose work is the universe; or whose act is all; or for his power of wondrous making; or for his likeness to Tvaṣṭṛ.

Bhāṣya Manuḥ: from thinking (‘there is no other thinker than he’, Bṛhadāraṇyaka 3.7.23); or the mantra, or Prajāpati. tvaṣṭā: at dissolution he makes all beings thin (tvakṣ ‘to pare’). sthaviṣṭhaḥ: most gross. sthaviraḥ: ancient (‘the bright name of this ancient one’, Ṛgveda), or a word of age. dhruvaḥ: being steady. ‘sthaviro dhruvaḥ’ is one name with its qualifier: the ancient, firm one.

अग्राह्यः शाश्वतः कृष्णो लोहिताक्षः प्रतर्दनः । प्रभूतस्त्रिककुब्धाम पवित्रं मङ्गलं परम् ॥ २० ॥
agrāhyaḥ śāśvataḥ kṛṣṇo lohitākṣaḥ pratardanaḥ | prabhūtas trikakubdhāma pavitraṃ maṅgalaṃ param || 20 ||
Padaccheda
अग्राह्यः शाश्वतः कृष्णः लोहित-अक्षः प्रतर्दनः प्रभूतः त्रि-ककुप्-धाम पवित्रम् मङ्गलम् परम्
agrāhyaḥ śāśvataḥ kṛṣṇaḥ lohita-akṣaḥ pratardanaḥ prabhūtaḥ tri-kakup-dhāma pavitram maṅgalam param
Word by word
(He is) Agrāhya, not grasped (by the organs); Śāśvata, Everlasting; Kṛṣṇa, (being and bliss); Lohitākṣa, the Red-eyed; Pratardana, the Destroyer (at dissolution); Prabhūta, rich (in knowledge and lordship); the Abode of the three regions; the Purifier; the supreme Auspicious.

Bhāṣya Agrāhyaḥ: not grasped by the organs of action (‘from which words turn back, with the mind, not reaching it’, Taittirīya 2.4). śāśvataḥ: he is at all times (‘everlasting, auspicious, unfallen’). kṛṣṇaḥ: he whose nature is being, consciousness and bliss (‘kṛṣ means being, ṇa means bliss; Viṣṇu, being both, is called Kṛṣṇa’, Vyāsa); or for his dark colour (‘because my colour is dark (kṛṣṇa), I am Kṛṣṇa, Arjuna’, Mahābhārata). lohitākṣaḥ: whose eyes are red. pratardanaḥ: he destroys beings at dissolution. prabhūtaḥ: full of the qualities of knowledge, lordship and the rest.

Bhāṣya Trikakubdhāma: the abode of the three regions, above, below and between; one name. pavitram: that by which, or he who, purifies; or a seer or deity, so named (Pāṇini 3.2.185–186). maṅgalaṃ param: ‘the Brahman that by mere remembrance drives away all that is inauspicious and spreads the auspicious is called maṅgala’ (Viṣṇu Purāṇa); or because he is auspicious; param: higher than all beings; one name with its qualifier.

ईशानः प्राणदः प्राणो ज्येष्ठः श्रेष्ठः प्रजापतिः । हिरण्यगर्भो भूगर्भो माधवो मधुसूदनः ॥ २१ ॥
īśānaḥ prāṇadaḥ prāṇo jyeṣṭhaḥ śreṣṭhaḥ prajāpatiḥ | hiraṇyagarbho bhūgarbho mādhavo madhusūdanaḥ || 21 ||
Padaccheda
ईशानः प्राण-दः प्राणः ज्येष्ठः श्रेष्ठः प्रजा-पतिः हिरण्य-गर्भः भू-गर्भः माधवः मधु-सूदनः
īśānaḥ prāṇa-daḥ prāṇaḥ jyeṣṭhaḥ śreṣṭhaḥ prajā-patiḥ hiraṇya-garbhaḥ bhū-garbhaḥ mādhavaḥ madhu-sūdanaḥ
Word by word
(He is) Īśāna, the Ruler (of all beings); Prāṇada, who gives (and takes) the breath; Prāṇa; Jyeṣṭha, the Eldest; Śreṣṭha, the Best; Prajāpati, the Lord of creatures; Hiraṇyagarbha; Bhūgarbha, who holds the earth within him; Mādhava, the Lord of Śrī; Madhusūdana, the Slayer of Madhu.

Bhāṣya Īśānaḥ: because he controls all beings. prāṇadaḥ: he gives the prāṇas, makes them move (‘who would breathe, who would live?’, Taittirīya 2.7); or as Time he cuts them off; or purifies them; or severs them. prāṇaḥ: he breathes: the knower of the field, or the supreme Self (‘the prāṇa of prāṇa’, Kena 1.2), or the chief prāṇa. jyeṣṭhaḥ: the eldest (Pāṇini 5.3.62); śreṣṭhaḥ: most to be praised (Pāṇini 5.3.60): ‘prāṇa is the eldest and the best’ (Chāndogya 5.1.1), or the chief prāṇa; or eldest as the cause of all, best as surpassing all. prajāpatiḥ: lord of all creatures, as Īśvara. hiraṇyagarbhaḥ: Brahmā, Viriñci, within the golden egg, whose self he is (‘Hiraṇyagarbha arose in the beginning’, Ṛgveda 10.121.1). bhūgarbhaḥ: who has the earth in his womb.

Bhāṣya Mādhavaḥ: the husband (dhava) of Mā, Śrī; or he who is taught in the Madhu-vidyā; or ‘know Mādhava, Bhārata, by silence, meditation and yoga’ (Vyāsa). madhusūdanaḥ: he slew the asura Madhu (‘the great asura Madhu, born from the dirt of the ear, Puruṣottama slew, doing a service to Brahmā; for that slaying gods, dānavas, men and seers call Janārdana Madhusūdana’, Mahābhārata).

ईश्वरो विक्रमी धन्वी मेधावी विक्रमः क्रमः । अनुत्तमो दुराधर्षः कृतज्ञः कृतिरात्मवान् ॥ २२ ॥
īśvaro vikramī dhanvī medhāvī vikramaḥ kramaḥ | anuttamo durādharṣaḥ kṛtajñaḥ kṛtir ātmavān || 22 ||
Padaccheda
ईश्वरः विक्रमी धन्वी मेधावी विक्रमः क्रमः अनुत्तमः दुराधर्षः कृत-ज्ञः कृतिः आत्मवान्
īśvaraḥ vikramī dhanvī medhāvī vikramaḥ kramaḥ anuttamaḥ durādharṣaḥ kṛta-jñaḥ kṛtiḥ ātmavān
Word by word
(He is) Īśvara, having all powers; Vikramī, the Valiant; Dhanvī, the Archer; Medhāvī, the Wise; Vikrama, who strode (over the world); Krama, the Step; Anuttama, the Unsurpassed; Durādharṣa, the Unassailable; Kṛtajña, who knows (the merit and demerit of) deeds; Kṛti, the deed (whose ground he is); Ātmavān, established in his own greatness.

Bhāṣya Īśvaraḥ: as having all powers. vikramī: from valour. dhanvī: having a bow (‘among the bearers of weapons I am Rāma’, Gītā 10.31). medhāvī: medhā is the power of holding many books. vikramaḥ: he strode over the whole world; or he moves by Garuḍa, the bird (vi). kramaḥ: from striding, or as the cause of order (‘Viṣṇu in the stride’, Manu 12.121). anuttamaḥ: than whom none is higher (‘than whom nothing is higher’, Śvetāśvatara 3.9; ‘none is equal to you, how a greater?’, Gītā 11.43). durādharṣaḥ: not to be assailed by gods and the rest.

Bhāṣya Kṛtajñaḥ: he knows the deeds of beings, merit and demerit; or he gives liberation even to those who offer a leaf or a flower. kṛtiḥ: human effort, or action; since he is the Self of all and its ground, it is figuratively called his. ātmavān: established in his own greatness (‘on what does he rest? On his own greatness’, Chāndogya 7.24.1).

सुरेशः शरणं शर्म विश्वरेताः प्रजाभवः । अहः संवत्सरो व्यालः प्रत्ययः सर्वदर्शनः ॥ २३ ॥
sureśaḥ śaraṇaṃ śarma viśvaretāḥ prajābhavaḥ | ahaḥ saṃvatsaro vyālaḥ pratyayaḥ sarvadarśanaḥ || 23 ||
Padaccheda
सुर-ईशः शरणम् शर्म विश्व-रेताः प्रजा-भवः अहः संवत्सरः व्यालः प्रत्ययः सर्व-दर्शनः
sura-īśaḥ śaraṇam śarma viśva-retāḥ prajā-bhavaḥ ahaḥ saṃvatsaraḥ vyālaḥ pratyayaḥ sarva-darśanaḥ
Word by word
(He is) Sureśa, the Lord of the gods; Śaraṇa, the Refuge; Śarma, Bliss; Viśvaretā, the seed of the universe; Prajābhava, from whom creatures arise; Ahaḥ, the Day (being light); Saṃvatsara, the Year (as Time); Vyāla, (as) a serpent (not to be seized); Pratyaya, Cognition; Sarvadarśana, whose eyes are everywhere.

Bhāṣya Sureśaḥ: lord of the suras, the gods; or lord of those who give well (su-rā). śaraṇam: as he takes away the pain of the afflicted. śarma: being of the nature of the supreme bliss. viśvaretāḥ: as the cause of the universe, its seed. prajābhavaḥ: from whom all creatures come forth. ahaḥ: being light by nature. saṃvatsaraḥ: Viṣṇu standing as Time is called the year. vyālaḥ: like a serpent (vyāla), he cannot be seized. pratyayaḥ: cognition, knowing (‘Brahman is consciousness’, Aitareya 3.1.3). sarvadarśanaḥ: all the eyes are his, since he is the Self of all (‘with eyes everywhere’, Śvetāśvatara 3.3).

अजः सर्वेश्वरः सिद्धः सिद्धिः सर्वादिरच्युतः । वृषाकपिरमेयात्मा सर्वयोगविनिःसृतः ॥ २४ ॥
ajaḥ sarveśvaraḥ siddhaḥ siddhiḥ sarvādir acyutaḥ | vṛṣākapir ameyātmā sarvayogaviniḥsṛtaḥ || 24 ||
Padaccheda
अजः सर्व-ईश्वरः सिद्धः सिद्धिः सर्व-आदिः अच्युतः वृषाकपिः अमेय-आत्मा सर्व-योग-विनिःसृतः
ajaḥ sarva-īśvaraḥ siddhaḥ siddhiḥ sarva-ādiḥ acyutaḥ vṛṣākapiḥ ameya-ātmā sarva-yoga-viniḥsṛtaḥ
Word by word
(He is) Aja, the Unborn; Sarveśvara, the Lord of all lords; Siddha, ever accomplished; Siddhi, Fulfilment (being consciousness in all things); Sarvādi, the first cause of all; Acyuta, the Unfallen; Vṛṣākapi, (dharma and the Boar); Ameyātmā, whose Self cannot be measured; Sarvayogaviniḥsṛta, free of every bond.

Bhāṣya Ajaḥ: he is not born (‘he was not born, nor will be’; ‘I was not born, am not born, will not be born; I am the knower of the field of all beings, so I am called aja’, Mahābhārata). sarveśvaraḥ: lord of all lords (‘he is the lord of all’). siddhaḥ: his nature is ever accomplished. siddhiḥ: being consciousness in all things, or being unsurpassed; the fruits of heaven and the rest perish, so they are no true fruit. sarvādiḥ: the first cause of all beings. acyutaḥ: by the strength of his own nature he has not fallen, does not and will not fall (‘everlasting, auspicious, unfallen’; ‘because I never fell before, I am Acyuta’, the Lord). Here the first hundred names are explained.

Bhāṣya Vṛṣākapiḥ: vṛṣa is dharma, which rains (varṣati) all desires; kapi is the Boar, who raised the earth from the water (ka); being both he is Vṛṣākapi (‘kapi is the Boar and best, vṛṣa is called dharma; so Prajāpati Kaśyapa called me Vṛṣākapi’, Mahābhārata). ameyātmā: his Self cannot be measured as ‘so much’. sarvayogaviniḥsṛtaḥ: he has gone out from every bond (‘this Puruṣa is unattached’, Bṛhadāraṇyaka 4.3.15); or he is beyond the yogas taught in the various śāstras.

वसुर्वसुमनाः सत्यः समात्मासंमितः समः । अमोघः पुण्डरीकाक्षो वृषकर्मा वृषाकृतिः ॥ २५ ॥
vasur vasumanāḥ satyaḥ samātmāsaṃmitaḥ samaḥ | amoghaḥ puṇḍarīkākṣo vṛṣakarmā vṛṣākṛtiḥ || 25 ||
Padaccheda
वसुः वसु-मनाः सत्यः सम-आत्मा असंमितः समः अमोघः पुण्डरीक-अक्षः वृष-कर्मा वृष-आकृतिः
vasuḥ vasu-manāḥ satyaḥ sama-ātmā asaṃmitaḥ samaḥ amoghaḥ puṇḍarīka-akṣaḥ vṛṣa-karmā vṛṣa-ākṛtiḥ
Word by word
(He is) Vasu, (in whom all dwell); Vasumanā, of excellent mind; Satya, the True; Samātmā, even-minded; Asaṃmita, not measured (by anything); Sama, ever the same; Amogha, Unfailing; Puṇḍarīkākṣa, (who pervades the lotus of the heart); Vṛṣakarmā, whose acts are dharma; Vṛṣākṛti, who takes form for dharma.

Bhāṣya Vasuḥ: all beings dwell (vasanti) in him, and he dwells in them; or Vasu as in ‘of the Vasus I am Pāvaka’ (Gītā 10.23). vasumanāḥ: vasu, wealth, here means excellence: his mind is excellent, being untroubled by attachment and aversion, the afflictions or pride. satyaḥ: the supreme Self, being of a nature that is not false (‘Brahman is truth, knowledge, infinite’, Taittirīya 2.1); or as being both the formed and the formless (Taittirīya 2.6); or sat (prāṇa), ti (food) and yam (the sun) (Bṛhadāraṇyaka 5.5.1); or as good among the good. samātmā: his mind is even, unspoiled by attachment and aversion; or the one Self, the same in all beings. asaṃmitaḥ: not measured by any of the things there are. samaḥ: the same at all times, free of all change; or he who is with Mā, Lakṣmī.

Bhāṣya Amoghaḥ: worshipped, praised or remembered, he gives the whole fruit and makes nothing vain; or his resolves come true (‘whose desires are true, whose resolves are true’, Chāndogya 8.1.5). puṇḍarīkākṣaḥ: he pervades (aśnute) the lotus of the heart and is perceived there (‘the lotus in the middle of the city’); or his eyes are shaped like the lotus. vṛṣakarmā: whose acts are dharma. vṛṣākṛtiḥ: whose form is taken for dharma (‘to establish dharma I come into being’, Gītā 4.8).

Note The edition's line reads samātmāsaṃmitaḥ; Śaṅkara takes it as samātmā and asaṃmitaḥ, two names.

रुद्रो बहुशिरा बभ्रुर्विश्वयोनिः शुचिश्रवाः । अमृतः शाश्वतस्थाणुर्वरारोहो महातपाः ॥ २६ ॥
rudro bahuśirā babhrur viśvayoniḥ śuciśravāḥ | amṛtaḥ śāśvatasthāṇur varāroho mahātapāḥ || 26 ||
Padaccheda
रुद्रः बहु-शिराः बभ्रुः विश्व-योनिः शुचि-श्रवाः अमृतः शाश्वत-स्थाणुः वर-आरोहः महा-तपाः
rudraḥ bahu-śirāḥ babhruḥ viśva-yoniḥ śuci-śravāḥ amṛtaḥ śāśvata-sthāṇuḥ vara-ārohaḥ mahā-tapāḥ
Word by word
(He is) Rudra, (who makes beings weep at dissolution); Bahuśirā, many-headed; Babhru, the Bearer (of the worlds); Viśvayoni, the source of the universe; Śuciśravā, whose names are pure; Amṛta, Deathless; everlasting and steadfast; Varāroha, the excellent height (from which there is no return); Mahātapā, whose knowledge (tapas) is great.

Bhāṣya Rudraḥ: at dissolution, withdrawing creatures, he makes them weep (rodayati); or he gives (rāti) rud; or drives away sorrow and its cause (‘because the Lord drives away sorrow and its cause he is called Rudra; so Śiva is the supreme cause’, Liṅga Purāṇa). bahuśirāḥ: having many heads (‘Puruṣa has a thousand heads’). babhruḥ: he bears (bibharti) the worlds. viśvayoniḥ: as the cause of the universe. śuciśravāḥ: whose names (śravas), worth hearing, are pure. amṛtaḥ: he has no death (‘ageless, deathless’). śāśvatasthāṇuḥ: everlasting and steady.

Bhāṣya Varārohaḥ: whose lap (āroha) is excellent; or in whom the ascent is excellent, since those who have ascended do not return (‘he does not return’, Chāndogya 8.15.1; ‘having reached which they do not return’, Gītā 8.21). mahātapāḥ: whose tapas, knowledge, with the things to be created as its object, is great (‘whose tapas consists of knowledge’, Muṇḍaka 1.1.9); or whose lordship or splendour is great.

सर्वगः सर्वविद्भानुर्विष्वक्सेनो जनार्दनः । वेदो वेदविदव्यङ्गो वेदाङ्गो वेदवित्कविः ॥ २७ ॥
sarvagaḥ sarvavid bhānur viṣvakseno janārdanaḥ | vedo vedavid avyaṅgo vedāṅgo vedavit kaviḥ || 27 ||
Padaccheda
सर्व-गः सर्व-वित् भानुः विष्वक्सेनः जनार्दनः वेदः वेद-वित् अव्यङ्गः वेद-अङ्गः वेद-वित् कविः
sarva-gaḥ sarva-vit bhānuḥ viṣvaksenaḥ janārdanaḥ vedaḥ veda-vit avyaṅgaḥ veda-aṅgaḥ veda-vit kaviḥ
Word by word
(He is) Sarvaga, everywhere; the all-knowing Light; Viṣvaksena, (before whom the hosts of demons scatter); Janārdana, (who strikes down the wicked, or to whom men pray); Veda, who makes known; Vedavit, who knows the Veda; Avyaṅga, without defect; Vedāṅga, whose limbs are the Vedas; Vedavit, who reflects on the Vedas; Kavi, the all-seeing.

Bhāṣya Sarvagaḥ: he goes everywhere, pervading all as the cause. sarvavid bhānuḥ: he knows or finds all, and he shines (‘him shining, all shines after’, Muṇḍaka 2.2.11; ‘the light in the sun’, Gītā 15.12): one name, ‘the all-knowing light’. viṣvaksenaḥ: before whom the army of the daityas flees on every side (viṣvak) at the mere stir of battle. janārdanaḥ: he afflicts (ardayati) wicked men, or sends them to hell and the rest; or men pray to (ardyate) him for the human ends, prosperity and the highest good.

Bhāṣya Vedaḥ: he makes known (vedayati) (‘out of compassion for them I destroy the darkness born of ignorance with the shining lamp of knowledge’, Gītā 10.11). vedavit: he knows the meaning of the Veda as it is (‘I am the maker of the Vedānta and the knower of the Veda’, Gītā 15.15; ‘all the Vedas … all sacrifices are Kṛṣṇa’, Mahābhārata). avyaṅgaḥ: full of knowledge and the rest, without defect; or he has no manifestation (‘this is unmanifest’, Gītā 2.25). vedāṅgaḥ: the Vedas are his limbs. vedavit: he reflects on (vindate) the Vedas. kaviḥ: he who sees beyond, the seer of all (‘there is no other seer’, Bṛhadāraṇyaka 3.7.23; ‘the seer, the wise’, Īśa 8).

लोकाध्यक्षः सुराध्यक्षो धर्माध्यक्षः कृताकृतः । चतुरात्मा चतुर्व्यूहश्चतुर्दंष्ट्रश्चतुर्भुजः ॥ २८ ॥
lokādhyakṣaḥ surādhyakṣo dharmādhyakṣaḥ kṛtākṛtaḥ | caturātmā caturvyūhaś caturdaṃṣṭraś caturbhujaḥ || 28 ||
Padaccheda
लोक-अध्यक्षः सुर-अध्यक्षः धर्म-अध्यक्षः कृत-अकृतः चतुर्-आत्मा चतुर्-व्यूहः चतुर्-दंष्ट्रः चतुर्-भुजः
loka-adhyakṣaḥ sura-adhyakṣaḥ dharma-adhyakṣaḥ kṛta-akṛtaḥ catur-ātmā catur-vyūhaḥ catur-daṃṣṭraḥ catur-bhujaḥ
Word by word
(He is) the Overseer of the worlds; the Overseer of the gods; the Overseer of dharma (and adharma); Kṛtākṛta, made (as effect) and unmade (as cause); Caturātmā, whose glories are four; Caturvyūha, of four emanations; Caturdaṃṣṭra, four-tusked; Caturbhuja, four-armed.

Bhāṣya Lokādhyakṣaḥ: he oversees all the worlds as their chief witness. surādhyakṣaḥ: overseer of the gods, the guardians of the worlds and the rest. dharmādhyakṣaḥ: he sees dharma and adharma directly, to give the fruit that fits. kṛtākṛtaḥ: made as the effect, unmade as the cause. caturātmā: in creation and the rest he has four separate glories (in creation Brahmā, Dakṣa and the others, time, and all creatures; in preservation Viṣṇu, Manu and the others, time, and all beings; in dissolution Rudra, time, Antaka and the rest, and all creatures: Viṣṇu Purāṇa). caturvyūhaḥ: ‘dividing himself fourfold as Vāsudeva and the others, Janārdana brings about creation and the rest’ (Vyāsa). caturdaṃṣṭraḥ: with four tusks, as Narasiṃha; or ‘tusk’ means horn, by likeness (‘four horns’, Ṛgveda 4.58.3). caturbhujaḥ: he has four arms.

भ्राजिष्णुर्भोजनं भोक्ता सहिष्णुर्जगदादिजः । अनघो विजयो जेता विश्वयोनिः पुनर्वसुः ॥ २९ ॥
bhrājiṣṇur bhojanaṃ bhoktā sahiṣṇur jagadādijaḥ | anagho vijayo jetā viśvayoniḥ punarvasuḥ || 29 ||
Padaccheda
भ्राजिष्णुः भोजनम् भोक्ता सहिष्णुः जगत्-आदि-जः अनघः विजयः जेता विश्व-योनिः पुनर्-वसुः
bhrājiṣṇuḥ bhojanam bhoktā sahiṣṇuḥ jagat-ādi-jaḥ anaghaḥ vijayaḥ jetā viśva-yoniḥ punar-vasuḥ
Word by word
(He is) Bhrājiṣṇu, pure radiance; Bhojana, the enjoyed (Prakṛti, his māyā); Bhoktā, the Enjoyer (as Puruṣa); Sahiṣṇu, who overcomes (Hiraṇyākṣa and the rest); Jagadādija, born (as Hiraṇyagarbha) at the beginning of the world; Anagha, Sinless; Vijaya, Victory; Jetā, the Conqueror; Viśvayoni, (the universe and its source); Punarvasu, who dwells in body after body.

Bhāṣya Bhrājiṣṇuḥ: being of the one taste of light. bhojanam: Prakṛti, māyā, as what is to be enjoyed, is called bhojana. bhoktā: as Puruṣa he enjoys it. sahiṣṇuḥ: he overcomes Hiraṇyākṣa and the others. jagadādijaḥ: as Hiraṇyagarbha he himself is born at the beginning of the world. anaghaḥ: no sin is in him (‘free of evil’, Chāndogya 8.1.5). vijayaḥ: he wins over the world by knowledge, dispassion, lordship and his other qualities. jetā: because he surpasses all beings by nature. viśvayoniḥ: the universe is his source, or he is the universe and its source. punarvasuḥ: he dwells again and again in bodies as the knower of the field.

उपेन्द्रो वामनः प्रांशुरमोघः शुचिरूर्जितः । अतीन्द्रः संग्रहः सर्गो धृतात्मा नियमो यमः ॥ ३० ॥
upendro vāmanaḥ prāṃśur amoghaḥ śucir ūrjitaḥ | atīndraḥ saṃgrahaḥ sargo dhṛtātmā niyamo yamaḥ || 30 ||
Padaccheda
उपेन्द्रः वामनः प्रांशुः अमोघः शुचिः ऊर्जितः अतीन्द्रः संग्रहः सर्गः धृत-आत्मा नियमः यमः
upendraḥ vāmanaḥ prāṃśuḥ amoghaḥ śuciḥ ūrjitaḥ atīndraḥ saṃgrahaḥ sargaḥ dhṛta-ātmā niyamaḥ yamaḥ
Word by word
(He is) Upendra, (Indra's younger brother, or Indra above Indra); Vāmana, the Dwarf; Prāṃśu, the Tall (striding the three worlds); Amogha, whose acts are never vain; Śuci, Pure; Ūrjita, Mighty; Atīndra, beyond Indra; Saṃgraha, who gathers all in; Sarga, Creation; Dhṛtātmā, who holds himself (one, unborn); Niyama, who sets creatures to their tasks; Yama, the inner Controller.

Bhāṣya Upendraḥ: he came to Indra (upa-indra) as his younger brother; or he is an Indra above (upari) (‘as you are placed above me as Indra by the cows, the gods on earth will sing of you, Kṛṣṇa, as Upendra’, Harivaṃśa). vāmanaḥ: in the form of a dwarf he begged of Bali; or he is to be worshipped (‘all the gods worship the dwarf seated in the middle’, Kaṭha 2.2.3). prāṃśuḥ: striding over the three worlds he became tall (the Harivaṃśa: when the water fell on his hand, the dwarf was no dwarf; he showed the form made of all the gods, earth his feet, heaven his head …). amoghaḥ: none of his acts is vain. śuciḥ: he purifies those who remember, praise and worship him (‘untouched, great, pure’). ūrjitaḥ: of the utmost strength.

Bhāṣya Atīndraḥ: he stands beyond Indra by his natural knowledge, lordship and the rest. saṃgrahaḥ: because he withdraws all into himself. sargaḥ: being the creation, or its cause. dhṛtātmā: who holds his self in one form, free of birth and the rest. niyamaḥ: he sets creatures to their own tasks. yamaḥ: he controls them from within.

वेद्यो वैद्यः सदायोगी वीरहा माधवो मधुः । अतीन्द्रियो महामायो महोत्साहो महाबलः ॥ ३१ ॥
vedyo vaidyaḥ sadāyogī vīrahā mādhavo madhuḥ | atīndriyo mahāmāyo mahotsāho mahābalaḥ || 31 ||
Padaccheda
वेद्यः वैद्यः सदा-योगी वीर-हा माधवः मधुः अतीन्द्रियः महा-मायः महा-उत्साहः महा-बलः
vedyaḥ vaidyaḥ sadā-yogī vīra-hā mādhavaḥ madhuḥ atīndriyaḥ mahā-māyaḥ mahā-utsāhaḥ mahā-balaḥ
Word by word
(He is) Vedya, to be known (by those who seek the highest good); Vaidya, who knows all sciences; Sadāyogī, ever manifest; Vīrahā, who slays the (demon) heroes; Mādhava, the Lord of knowledge (mā); Madhu, sweet as honey; Atīndriya, beyond the senses; Mahāmāya, the great wielder of māyā; Mahotsāha, of great energy; Mahābala, of great strength.

Bhāṣya Vedyaḥ: worthy to be known by those who seek the highest good. vaidyaḥ: he knows all sciences. sadāyogī: his nature is always manifest. vīrahā: to protect dharma he slays the heroic asuras. mādhavaḥ: the lord of mā, knowledge (‘Hari's knowledge is called mā; since you are its lord, you are named Mādhava; dhava means lord’, Harivaṃśa). madhuḥ: as honey gives the highest delight, so does he. atīndriyaḥ: being without sound and the rest, not the object of the senses (‘soundless, touchless’, Kaṭha 1.3.15). mahāmāyaḥ: he works māyā even on the workers of māyā (‘my māyā is hard to cross’, Gītā 7.14). mahotsāhaḥ: ever ready for the creation, preservation and dissolution of the world. mahābalaḥ: strongest of the strong.

महाबुद्धिर्महावीर्यो महाशक्तिर्महाद्युतिः । अनिर्देश्यवपुः श्रीमानमेयात्मा महाद्रिधृक् ॥ ३२ ॥
mahābuddhir mahāvīryo mahāśaktir mahādyutiḥ | anirdeśyavapuḥ śrīmān ameyātmā mahādridhṛk || 32 ||
Padaccheda
महा-बुद्धिः महा-वीर्यः महा-शक्तिः महा-द्युतिः अनिर्देश्य-वपुः श्रीमान् अमेय-आत्मा महा-अद्रि-धृक्
mahā-buddhiḥ mahā-vīryaḥ mahā-śaktiḥ mahā-dyutiḥ anirdeśya-vapuḥ śrīmān ameya-ātmā mahā-adri-dhṛk
Word by word
(He is) Mahābuddhi, of great understanding; Mahāvīrya, whose power (ignorance, the cause of the great world) is great; Mahāśakti, of great might; Mahādyuti, of great light (within and without); Anirdeśyavapu, whose form cannot be pointed out; Śrīmān, of full lordship; Ameyātmā, whose understanding none can measure; Mahādridhṛk, who held up the great mountain (Mandara and Govardhana).

Bhāṣya Mahābuddhiḥ: wisest of the wise. mahāvīryaḥ: his power, ignorance, the cause from which the great (mahat) arises, is great. mahāśaktiḥ: of great power and ability. mahādyutiḥ: his light within and without is great (‘self-luminous’, Bṛhadāraṇyaka 4.3.9; ‘the light of lights’, Muṇḍaka 2.2.10). anirdeśyavapuḥ: his form cannot be pointed out to another as ‘this, that’, being known only in itself. śrīmān: whose glory, lordship, is entire. ameyātmā: his understanding (ātman) cannot be measured by any being. mahādridhṛk: he held the great mountain, Mandara at the churning of the nectar and Govardhana to protect the cows.

महेष्वासो महीभर्ता श्रीनिवासः सतां गतिः । अनिरुद्धः सुरानन्दो गोविन्दो गोविदां पतिः ॥ ३३ ॥
maheṣvāso mahībhartā śrīnivāsaḥ satāṃ gatiḥ | aniruddhaḥ surānando govindo govidāṃ patiḥ || 33 ||
Padaccheda
महा-इष्वासः मही-भर्ता श्री-निवासः सताम् गतिः अनिरुद्धः सुर-आनन्दः गोविन्दः गो-विदाम् पतिः
mahā-iṣvāsaḥ mahī-bhartā śrī-nivāsaḥ satām gatiḥ aniruddhaḥ sura-ānandaḥ govindaḥ go-vidām patiḥ
Word by word
(He is) Maheṣvāsa, the great Archer; Mahībhartā, who bore the earth (out of the one ocean); Śrīnivāsa, in whose breast Śrī dwells; the Refuge of the good; Aniruddha, never obstructed; Surānanda, the gods' delight; Govinda; the Lord of those who know speech (the Veda).

Bhāṣya Maheṣvāsaḥ: whose shooting of arrows is great. mahībhartā: he bore the goddess Earth, sunk in the one ocean. śrīnivāsaḥ: on whose breast Śrī dwells, never leaving. satāṃ gatiḥ: the means of the human ends for the good, the followers of the Veda. aniruddhaḥ: not obstructed by anyone in his appearances. surānandaḥ: he gladdens the gods. govindaḥ: ‘because I found (avindam) the earth (go) lost in the cave, the gods praised me as Govinda’ (Mokṣadharma); ‘I am Indra of the gods; you have become the Indra of cows (go); men will praise you as Govinda for ever’; ‘go is speech; since you find it, the sages call you Govinda’ (Harivaṃśa). govidāṃ patiḥ: go is speech; those who find it are govidaḥ; he is their lord in a special way.

मरीचिर्दमनो हंसः सुपर्णो भुजगोत्तमः । हिरण्यनाभः सुतपाः पद्मनाभः प्रजापतिः ॥ ३४ ॥
marīcir damano haṃsaḥ suparṇo bhujagottamaḥ | hiraṇyanābhaḥ sutapāḥ padmanābhaḥ prajāpatiḥ || 34 ||
Padaccheda
मरीचिः दमनः हंसः सुपर्णः भुजग-उत्तमः हिरण्य-नाभः सु-तपाः पद्म-नाभः प्रजा-पतिः
marīciḥ damanaḥ haṃsaḥ suparṇaḥ bhujaga-uttamaḥ hiraṇya-nābhaḥ su-tapāḥ padma-nābhaḥ prajā-patiḥ
Word by word
(He is) Marīci, the light (of the luminous); Damana, who tames (creatures who stray); Haṃsa, (who slays the fear of the round for one who thinks ‘I am he’); Suparṇa, fair-winged; the best of serpents; Hiraṇyanābha, whose navel is fair as gold; Sutapā, (as Nara and Nārāyaṇa) of good austerity; Padmanābha, (shining in the lotus of the heart); Prajāpati, the father of creatures.

Bhāṣya Marīciḥ: the light even of those that shine (‘I am the light of the luminous’, Gītā 10.36). damanaḥ: as Vaivasvata and others he tames creatures that stray from their duties. haṃsaḥ: for one who thinks ‘I am he’ (ahaṃ saḥ) he slays (hanti) the fear of the round; or he goes (hanti) in all bodies (‘the swan dwelling in the bright’, Kaṭha 2.2.2). suparṇaḥ: of fair wings (‘two birds of fair wings’, Muṇḍaka 3.1.1). bhujagottamaḥ: the best of those that move on their arms (serpents). hiraṇyanābhaḥ: whose navel is fair like gold, or good and lovely. sutapāḥ: in the hermitage of Badarī, as Nara and Nārāyaṇa, he practises good austerity (‘one-pointedness of mind and senses is the highest austerity’). padmanābhaḥ: whose navel is round like a lotus; or who shines in the middle of the lotus of the heart. prajāpatiḥ: the father of creatures.

अमृत्युः सर्वदृक्सिंहः संधाता संधिमान्स्थिरः । अजो दुर्मर्षणः शास्ता विश्रुतात्मा सुरारिहा ॥ ३५ ॥
amṛtyuḥ sarvadṛk siṃhaḥ saṃdhātā saṃdhimān sthiraḥ | ajo durmarṣaṇaḥ śāstā viśrutātmā surārihā || 35 ||
Padaccheda
अमृत्युः सर्व-दृक् सिंहः संधाता संधिमान् स्थिरः अजः दुर्मर्षणः शास्ता विश्रुत-आत्मा सुर-अरि-हा
amṛtyuḥ sarva-dṛk siṃhaḥ saṃdhātā saṃdhimān sthiraḥ ajaḥ durmarṣaṇaḥ śāstā viśruta-ātmā sura-ari-hā
Word by word
(He is) Amṛtyu, without death or its cause; Sarvadṛk, who sees (all deeds); Siṃha, (who slays); Saṃdhātā, who joins men to the fruits of their acts; Saṃdhimān, (himself the one joined, the enjoyer); Sthira, steady; Aja, (who goes and casts out); Durmarṣaṇa, not to be borne (by the demons); Śāstā, the Teacher; Viśrutātmā, whose nature (truth, knowledge) is renowned; Surārihā, slayer of the gods' foes.

Bhāṣya Amṛtyuḥ: neither death, destruction, nor its cause is in him. sarvadṛk: by his natural knowing he sees all the deeds, done and undone, of beings. siṃhaḥ: he slays (hinasti); the form is correct as one of the pṛṣodara group. Here the second hundred names are explained. saṃdhātā: he joins men to the fruits of their acts. saṃdhimān: he himself is also the one so joined, the enjoyer. sthiraḥ: being ever of one form. ajaḥ: he goes (aj), or casts out. durmarṣaṇaḥ: the demons cannot bear him. śāstā: he instructs all through scripture, tradition and the rest. viśrutātmā: his self, truth, knowledge and the rest, is especially heard of. surārihā: as the slayer of the enemies of the gods.

Note The edition's p. 70 is not inked at the ends of its last three lines; the lost words of the glosses on saṃdhimān, aja and durmarṣaṇa are supplied from the Gita Press and Mysore editions, which agree: ‘he is also the enjoyer of the fruits: saṃdhimān’; ‘he goes (ajati), or casts out: aja’; ‘he cannot be borne by the Dānavas and the rest: durmarṣaṇa’.

गुरुर्गुरुतमो धाम सत्यः सत्यपराक्रमः । निमिषोऽनिमिषः स्रग्वी वाचस्पतिरुदारधीः ॥ ३६ ॥
gurur gurutamo dhāma satyaḥ satyaparākramaḥ | nimiṣo 'nimiṣaḥ sragvī vācaspatir udāradhīḥ || 36 ||
Padaccheda
गुरुः गुरु-तमः धाम सत्यः सत्य-पराक्रमः निमिषः अनिमिषः स्रग्वी वाचः-पतिः उदार-धीः
guruḥ guru-tamaḥ dhāma satyaḥ satya-parākramaḥ nimiṣaḥ animiṣaḥ sragvī vācaḥ-patiḥ udāra-dhīḥ
Word by word
(He is) Guru, the Teacher (of all knowledge); Gurutama, the greatest Teacher (even of Viriñci); Dhāma, Light; Satya, Truth; Satyaparākrama, of true valour; Nimiṣa, (his eyes closed in the sleep of yoga); Animiṣa, ever awake; Sragvī, who wears (the Vaijayantī) garland; Vācaspati, lord of knowledge, of understanding that grasps all.

Bhāṣya Guruḥ: as the teacher of all branches of knowledge, or as the father of all. gurutamaḥ: he hands on the knowledge of Brahman even to Viriñci and the others (‘who made Brahmā first’, Śvetāśvatara 6.18). dhāma: light (‘Nārāyaṇa, the supreme light’); or the abode of all desires (‘the supreme Brahman, the supreme abode’). satyaḥ: being dharma in the form of true speech (‘so they call truth the highest’); or the true of the true (‘the prāṇas are true; he is their truth’, Bṛhadāraṇyaka 2.1.20). satyaparākramaḥ: whose valour is not false. nimiṣaḥ: his eyes are closed as he delights in the sleep of yoga. animiṣaḥ: ever awake by nature; or as the Fish, or as the Self.

Bhāṣya Sragvī: he always wears the garland called Vaijayantī, made of the subtle elements. vācaspatir udāradhīḥ: the lord of speech, knowledge, whose understanding grasps all things; one name.

अग्रणीर्ग्रामणीः श्रीमान्न्यायो नेता समीरणः । सहस्रमूर्धा विश्वात्मा सहस्राक्षः सहस्रपात् ॥ ३७ ॥
agraṇīr grāmaṇīḥ śrīmān nyāyo netā samīraṇaḥ | sahasramūrdhā viśvātmā sahasrākṣaḥ sahasrapāt || 37 ||
Padaccheda
अग्रणीः ग्रामणीः श्रीमान् न्यायः नेता समीरणः सहस्र-मूर्धा विश्व-आत्मा सहस्र-अक्षः सहस्र-पात्
agraṇīḥ grāmaṇīḥ śrīmān nyāyaḥ netā samīraṇaḥ sahasra-mūrdhā viśva-ātmā sahasra-akṣaḥ sahasra-pāt
Word by word
(He is) Agraṇī, who leads (seekers of liberation) to the highest state; Grāmaṇī, the leader of the host of beings; Śrīmān, of surpassing radiance; Nyāya, reason (that leads to non-difference); Netā, who drives the machine of the world; Samīraṇa, (as breath) moving beings; thousand-headed; the Self of the universe; thousand-eyed; thousand-footed.

Bhāṣya Agraṇīḥ: he leads seekers of liberation to the highest state. grāmaṇīḥ: as the leader of the host (grāma) of beings. śrīmān: his radiance surpasses all. nyāyaḥ: reasoning that supports the means of knowledge and leads to non-difference. netā: he works the machine of the world. samīraṇaḥ: as breath he makes beings move. sahasramūrdhā, sahasrākṣaḥ, sahasrapāt: having a thousand heads, eyes and feet (‘Puruṣa has a thousand heads, a thousand eyes, a thousand feet’, Ṛgveda 10.90.1). viśvātmā: the self of the universe.

आवर्तनो निवृत्तात्मा संवृतः सम्प्रमर्दनः । अहःसंवर्तको वह्निरनिलो धरणीधरः ॥ ३८ ॥
āvartano nivṛttātmā saṃvṛtaḥ sampramardanaḥ | ahaḥsaṃvartako vahnir anilo dharaṇīdharaḥ || 38 ||
Padaccheda
आवर्तनः निवृत्त-आत्मा संवृतः सम्प्रमर्दनः अहः-संवर्तकः वह्निः अनिलः धरणी-धरः
āvartanaḥ nivṛtta-ātmā saṃvṛtaḥ sampramardanaḥ ahaḥ-saṃvartakaḥ vahniḥ anilaḥ dharaṇī-dharaḥ
Word by word
(He is) Āvartana, who turns the wheel of the round; Nivṛttātmā, whose nature is free of the bonds of the round; Saṃvṛta, covered (by ignorance); Sampramardana, who crushes (through Rudra and Time); Ahaḥsaṃvartaka, (the sun) who brings round the day; Vahni, who bears (the oblations); Anila, (without abode, beginning or taking); Dharaṇīdhara, who holds up the earth.

Bhāṣya Āvartanaḥ: he turns the wheel of the round. nivṛttātmā: his nature is turned away from the bonds of the round. saṃvṛtaḥ: covered by the ignorance that conceals. sampramardanaḥ: he crushes well through his glories, Rudra, Time and the rest. ahaḥsaṃvartakaḥ: the sun, who rightly sets the days going. vahniḥ: from bearing (vahana). anilaḥ: having no abode (nilaya); or being without beginning; or not taking; or as breath (ana). dharaṇīdharaḥ: as Śeṣa and as the Boar he holds up the earth.

सुप्रसादः प्रसन्नात्मा विश्वधृग्विश्वभुग्विभुः । सत्कर्ता सत्कृतः साधुर्जह्नुर्नारायणो नरः ॥ ३९ ॥
suprasādaḥ prasannātmā viśvadhṛg viśvabhug vibhuḥ | satkartā satkṛtaḥ sādhur jahnur nārāyaṇo naraḥ || 39 ||
Padaccheda
सु-प्रसादः प्रसन्न-आत्मा विश्व-धृक् विश्व-भुक् विभुः सत्-कर्ता सत्-कृतः साधुः जह्नुः नारायणः नरः
su-prasādaḥ prasanna-ātmā viśva-dhṛk viśva-bhuk vibhuḥ sat-kartā sat-kṛtaḥ sādhuḥ jahnuḥ nārāyaṇaḥ naraḥ
Word by word
(He is) Suprasāda, whose grace (gives liberation even to Śiśupāla and other wrongdoers); Prasannātmā, whose mind is clear; Viśvadhṛk, who masters the world; Viśvabhuk, who enjoys and protects the world; Vibhu, (becoming manifold as Hiraṇyagarbha and the rest); Satkartā, who honours; Satkṛta, honoured by the honoured; Sādhu, the good; Jahnu, who takes away (beings at dissolution); Nārāyaṇa, the abode of all that comes from Nara; Nara, the Leader.

Bhāṣya Suprasādaḥ: his grace is good, for he gives liberation even to those who wronged him, Śiśupāla and the others. prasannātmā: his inner organ is unclouded by rajas and tamas; or he is clear by nature, compassionate; or all his desires are attained. viśvadhṛk: he masters (dhṛṣ) the world. viśvabhuk: he enjoys the world, or protects it. vibhuḥ: he becomes manifold as Hiraṇyagarbha and the rest. satkartā: he honours, worships. satkṛtaḥ: honoured even by the honoured. sādhuḥ: as acting by right; or as he accomplishes; or he accomplishes whatever is to be accomplished. jahnuḥ: at dissolution he takes beings away; or he leaves aside the ignorant and those without devotion, and leads others to the supreme.

Bhāṣya Nārāyaṇaḥ: nara is the Self; the effects born from it, space and the rest, are nāra; he pervades them as their cause, so they are his abode (ayana) (‘whatever in the world is seen or heard, pervading all that within and without, Nārāyaṇa stands’; ‘the elements born from Nara are called nāra; they are his abode, so he is Nārāyaṇa’, Mahābhārata); or he is the abode of the nāras, the jīvas, at dissolution (Taittirīya 3.1; Brahmavaivarta); or ‘the waters are called nārāḥ, the children of Nara; since they were his first abode, he is Nārāyaṇa’ (Manu 1.10). ‘Homage to Nārāyaṇa’ is the mantra that at once removes the dread poison of the round (Narasiṃha Purāṇa). naraḥ: ‘the eternal supreme Self is called nara because he leads (nayati)’ (Vyāsa).

असंख्येयोऽप्रमेयात्मा विशिष्टः शिष्टकृच्छुचिः । सिद्धार्थः सिद्धसंकल्पः सिद्धिदः सिद्धिसाधनः ॥ ४० ॥
asaṃkhyeyo 'prameyātmā viśiṣṭaḥ śiṣṭakṛc chuciḥ | siddhārthaḥ siddhasaṃkalpaḥ siddhidaḥ siddhisādhanaḥ || 40 ||
Padaccheda
असंख्येयः अप्रमेय-आत्मा विशिष्टः शिष्ट-कृत् शुचिः सिद्ध-अर्थः सिद्ध-संकल्पः सिद्धि-दः सिद्धि-साधनः
asaṃkhyeyaḥ aprameya-ātmā viśiṣṭaḥ śiṣṭa-kṛt śuciḥ siddha-arthaḥ siddha-saṃkalpaḥ siddhi-daḥ siddhi-sādhanaḥ
Word by word
(He is) Asaṃkhyeya, in whom there is no number (of name and form); Aprameyātmā, whose nature is beyond measure; Viśiṣṭa, surpassing all; Śiṣṭakṛt, who gives the command (and protects the good); Śuci, stainless; Siddhārtha, whose aims are attained; Siddhasaṃkalpa, whose resolves come true; Siddhida, who gives fulfilment (as each has earned); Siddhisādhana, the means of fulfilment.

Bhāṣya Asaṃkhyeyaḥ: in him there is no number, no difference of name and form. aprameyātmā: whose nature cannot be measured. viśiṣṭaḥ: because he surpasses all. śiṣṭakṛt: he makes the command; or he makes, that is, protects, the good; or, as a root of general sense may stand for a particular one, ‘do’ for ‘bring wood’. śuciḥ: stainless. siddhārthaḥ: whose desired aims are already achieved (‘whose desires are true’, Chāndogya 8.1.5). siddhasaṃkalpaḥ: whose resolves are fulfilled (‘whose resolves are true’). siddhidaḥ: he gives to doers the fruit that fits their part. siddhisādhanaḥ: as the means of accomplishment.

वृषाही वृषभो विष्णुर्वृषपर्वा वृषोदरः । वर्धनो वर्धमानश्च विविक्तः श्रुतिसागरः ॥ ४१ ॥
vṛṣāhī vṛṣabho viṣṇur vṛṣaparvā vṛṣodaraḥ | vardhano vardhamānaś ca viviktaḥ śrutisāgaraḥ || 41 ||
Padaccheda
वृषाही वृषभः विष्णुः वृष-पर्वा वृष-उदरः वर्धनः वर्धमानः च विविक्तः श्रुति-सागरः
vṛṣāhī vṛṣabhaḥ viṣṇuḥ vṛṣa-parvā vṛṣa-udaraḥ vardhanaḥ vardhamānaḥ ca viviktaḥ śruti-sāgaraḥ
Word by word
(He is) Vṛṣāhī, (to whom the twelve-day rite, dharma, belongs); Vṛṣabha, who showers desires on devotees; Viṣṇu, (the Strider); Vṛṣaparvā, whose steps (to the highest abode) are dharma; Vṛṣodara, whose womb showers forth creatures; Vardhana, who makes grow; Vardhamāna, who grows (as the manifold); and Vivikta, who stands apart; Śrutisāgara, in whom the Vedas are laid as in an ocean.

Bhāṣya Vṛṣāhī: vṛṣa is dharma, merit; the day (ahan) that is dharma is the rite of twelve days and the like, vṛṣāha (with the compound-final suffix ṭac, Pāṇini 5.4.91); he has it (-in). vṛṣabhaḥ: he showers (varṣati) desires on his devotees. viṣṇuḥ: ‘Viṣṇu, from striding’ (Vyāsa). vṛṣaparvā: the steps of dharma, as stairs to the supreme abode. vṛṣodaraḥ: his belly as it were rains creatures. vardhanaḥ: he makes grow. vardhamānaḥ: he grows as the manifold. viviktaḥ: though growing so, he stands apart. śrutisāgaraḥ: the Vedas are laid in him as in the ocean.

सुभुजो दुर्धरो वाग्मी महेन्द्रो वसुदो वसुः । नैकरूपो बृहद्रूपः शिपिविष्टः प्रकाशनः ॥ ४२ ॥
subhujo durdharo vāgmī mahendro vasudo vasuḥ | naikarūpo bṛhadrūpaḥ śipiviṣṭaḥ prakāśanaḥ || 42 ||
Padaccheda
सु-भुजः दुर्धरः वाग्मी महा-इन्द्रः वसु-दः वसुः नैक-रूपः बृहत्-रूपः शिपिविष्टः प्रकाशनः
su-bhujaḥ durdharaḥ vāgmī mahā-indraḥ vasu-daḥ vasuḥ naika-rūpaḥ bṛhat-rūpaḥ śipiviṣṭaḥ prakāśanaḥ
Word by word
(He is) Subhuja, whose fair arms guard the world; Durdhara, (bearing what none else can bear, and hard to hold in meditation); Vāgmī, from whom the Veda came forth; Mahendra, lord even of lords; Vasuda, giver of wealth; Vasu, (himself the wealth given); Naikarūpa, of many forms; Bṛhadrūpa, of vast form (the Boar); Śipiviṣṭa, who abides in the victims (as the sacrifice) or in the rays; Prakāśana, who lights all.

Bhāṣya Subhujaḥ: his fair arms protect the world. durdharaḥ: he bears the earth and the rest, which others cannot bear, and none can bear him; or he is held in the heart only with difficulty by seekers of liberation in meditation. vāgmī: from him came the speech that is the Veda. mahendraḥ: great and Indra, lord even of the lords. vasudaḥ: giver of wealth (‘eater of food, giver of wealth’, Bṛhadāraṇyaka 4.4.24). vasuḥ: he is himself the wealth given; or he covers (vas) his own nature by māyā; or he is the wind, dwelling only in the mid-region (‘Vasu dwelling in the mid-region’, Kaṭha 2.2.2). naikarūpaḥ: he has not one form (‘Indra by his māyās goes in many forms’, Bṛhadāraṇyaka 2.5.19). bṛhadrūpaḥ: whose form, the Boar and the rest, is vast.

Bhāṣya Śipiviṣṭaḥ: śipi are the victims; he dwells in them as the sacrifice (‘Viṣṇu is the sacrifice; the victims are śipi’); or śipi are the rays, and he has entered them (a verse he cites: water is called śi; the rays that drink and guard it are śipi). prakāśanaḥ: his nature is to illumine all.

ओजस्तेजोद्युतिधरः प्रकाशात्मा प्रतापनः । ऋद्धः स्पष्टाक्षरो मन्त्रश्चन्द्रांशुर्भास्करद्युतिः ॥ ४३ ॥
ojastejodyutidharaḥ prakāśātmā pratāpanaḥ | ṛddhaḥ spaṣṭākṣaro mantraś candrāṃśur bhāskaradyutiḥ || 43 ||
Padaccheda
ओजः-तेजः-द्युति-धरः प्रकाश-आत्मा प्रतापनः ऋद्धः स्पष्ट-अक्षरः मन्त्रः चन्द्र-अंशुः भास्कर-द्युतिः
ojaḥ-tejaḥ-dyuti-dharaḥ prakāśa-ātmā pratāpanaḥ ṛddhaḥ spaṣṭa-akṣaraḥ mantraḥ candra-aṃśuḥ bhāskara-dyutiḥ
Word by word
(He is) the Bearer of vigour, valour and light; Prakāśātmā, whose nature is light; Pratāpana, who heats the world (through the sun); Ṛddha, rich (in dharma, knowledge, dispassion); Spaṣṭākṣara, whose syllable (Om) is clear; Mantra, (the Ṛc, Yajus and Sāman); Candrāṃśu, (cooling) as moonbeams; Bhāskaradyuti, bright as the sun.

Bhāṣya Ojastejodyutidharaḥ: ojas is the strength of life, tejas valour and like qualities, dyuti light; he bears them. Or ojas and tejas are two names (‘of the strong I am the strength … the light of the luminous’, Gītā 7.10–11), and dyutidhara, who bears the light of knowledge. prakāśātmā: whose self is light. pratāpanaḥ: through his glories, the sun and the rest, he heats the world. ṛddhaḥ: full of dharma, knowledge, dispassion and the rest. spaṣṭākṣaraḥ: whose syllable, the high-pitched Om, is clear. mantraḥ: the mantra in the form of Ṛc, Yajus and Sāman; or as known from the mantras. candrāṃśuḥ: like the moon's rays he gladdens those whose minds are burnt by the sharp sun of the round. bhāskaradyutiḥ: like the sun in splendour.

अमृतांशूद्भवो भानुः शशबिन्दुः सुरेश्वरः । औषधं जगतः सेतुः सत्यधर्मपराक्रमः ॥ ४४ ॥
amṛtāṃśūdbhavo bhānuḥ śaśabinduḥ sureśvaraḥ | auṣadhaṃ jagataḥ setuḥ satyadharmaparākramaḥ || 44 ||
Padaccheda
अमृत-अंशु-उद्भवः भानुः शश-बिन्दुः सुर-ईश्वरः औषधम् जगतः सेतुः सत्य-धर्म-पराक्रमः
amṛta-aṃśu-udbhavaḥ bhānuḥ śaśa-binduḥ sura-īśvaraḥ auṣadham jagataḥ setuḥ satya-dharma-parākramaḥ
Word by word
(He is) Amṛtāṃśūdbhava, from whom the moon arose (at the churning); Bhānu, who shines; Śaśabindu, (the moon, nourishing creatures); Sureśvara, the Lord of the gods; Auṣadha, the medicine (for the disease of the round); the Bridge of the world; Satyadharmaparākrama, whose dharma (knowledge and other qualities) and valour are true.

Bhāṣya Amṛtāṃśūdbhavaḥ: from whom the moon, of nectar rays, arose when the ocean was churned. bhānuḥ: he shines (‘him shining, all shines after’, Muṇḍaka 2.2.11). śaśabinduḥ: the moon, marked with a hare, who nourishes creatures (‘becoming the moon, full of sap, I nourish all plants’, Gītā 15.13). sureśvaraḥ: lord of the gods, and of those who give well. auṣadham: as the medicine for the disease of the round. jagataḥ setuḥ: as the means of crossing; or a bridge, a dike, that keeps the classes and stages of life from mingling (‘he is the bridge, the dike, that these worlds may not mingle’, Bṛhadāraṇyaka 4.4.22). satyadharmaparākramaḥ: whose dharma, knowledge and other qualities, and valour, are true.

भूतभव्यभवन्नाथः पवनः पावनोऽनलः । कामहा कामकृत्कान्तः कामः कामप्रदः प्रभुः ॥ ४५ ॥
bhūtabhavyabhavannāthaḥ pavanaḥ pāvano 'nalaḥ | kāmahā kāmakṛt kāntaḥ kāmaḥ kāmapradaḥ prabhuḥ || 45 ||
Padaccheda
भूत-भव्य-भवत्-नाथः पवनः पावनः अनलः काम-हा काम-कृत् कान्तः कामः काम-प्रदः प्रभुः
bhūta-bhavya-bhavat-nāthaḥ pavanaḥ pāvanaḥ analaḥ kāma-hā kāma-kṛt kāntaḥ kāmaḥ kāma-pradaḥ prabhuḥ
Word by word
(He is) the Lord of the beings of past, future and present; Pavana, who purifies; Pāvana, who makes (the wind) blow; Anala, (the jīva, taking the breaths as self); Kāmahā, who destroys the desires (of seekers of liberation); Kāmakṛt, who fulfils the desires (of the good); Kānta, most beautiful; Kāma, the one desired; Kāmaprada, who gives desires abundantly; Prabhu, (eminent in being).

Bhāṣya Bhūtabhavyabhavannāthaḥ: lord of the hosts of beings, past, future and present; they pray to him; or he afflicts them (upatapati), rules them, governs them. pavanaḥ: he purifies (‘of purifiers I am the wind’, Gītā 10.31). pāvanaḥ: he makes the wind move (‘from fear of him the wind blows’, Taittirīya 2.8). analaḥ: the jīva, who takes (lāti) the breaths (ana) as himself; or from the root of smell with the negative (‘without smell, without taste’, Kaṭha 1.3.15); or for whom there is no ‘enough’. kāmahā: he destroys the desires of devotees who seek liberation, and of those who do harm. kāmakṛt: he fulfils the desires of the sattvic; or as the father of Kāma, Pradyumna. kāntaḥ: most beautiful. kāmaḥ: he is desired by those who seek the human ends. kāmapradaḥ: he gives desires to devotees abundantly. prabhuḥ: from eminent being.

युगादिकृद्युगावर्तो नैकमायो महाशनः । अदृश्यो व्यक्तरूपश्च सहस्रजिदनन्तजित् ॥ ४६ ॥
yugādikṛd yugāvarto naikamāyo mahāśanaḥ | adṛśyo vyaktarūpaś ca sahasrajid anantajit || 46 ||
Padaccheda
युग-आदि-कृत् युग-आवर्तः नैक-मायः महा-अशनः अदृश्यः व्यक्त-रूपः च सहस्र-जित् अनन्त-जित्
yuga-ādi-kṛt yuga-āvartaḥ naika-māyaḥ mahā-aśanaḥ adṛśyaḥ vyakta-rūpaḥ ca sahasra-jit ananta-jit
Word by word
(He is) Yugādikṛt, the maker of the ages and their beginnings; Yugāvarta, who as Time turns the ages; Naikamāya, whose māyās are many; Mahāśana, the great Eater (at the end of the kalpa); Adṛśya, beyond the senses; and Vyaktarūpa, manifest (in the gross form, and to yogins); Sahasrajit, who conquers thousands (of asuras); Anantajit, who conquers everywhere by unthinkable power.

Bhāṣya Yugādikṛt: as the maker of time and its divisions, the ages; or who makes the beginning of the ages.
