सुवर्ण-वर्णः हेम-अङ्गः वर-अङ्गः चन्दन-अङ्गदी वीर-हा विषमः शून्यः घृत-आशीः अचलः चलः
suvarṇa-varṇaḥ hema-aṅgaḥ vara-aṅgaḥ candana-aṅgadī vīra-hā viṣamaḥ śūnyaḥ ghṛta-āśīḥ acalaḥ calaḥ
Word by word
(He is) Suvarṇavarṇa, golden of hue; Hemāṅga, whose body is like gold (the golden Puruṣa in the sun); Varāṅga, of fine limbs; Candanāṅgadī, adorned with delightful armlets; Vīrahā, slayer of the heroic asuras; Viṣama, who has no equal; Śūnya, (as if empty, being free of all distinctions); Ghṛtāśī, whose wishes have dropped away; Acala, never moving (from his nature, power or qualities); Cala, moving (as the wind).

Bhāṣya Suvarṇavarṇaḥ: his colour is like gold (‘when the seer sees the golden-hued’, Muṇḍaka 3.1.3). hemāṅgaḥ: his body is like gold (‘the golden Puruṣa within the sun’, Chāndogya 1.6.6). varāṅgaḥ: his limbs are fine. candanāṅgadī: adorned with delightful (candana) armlets. vīrahā: to protect dharma he slays the heroic chiefs of the asuras. viṣamaḥ: none is like him, being unlike all (Gītā 11.43). śūnyaḥ: being free of all distinctions, he is like a void. ghṛtāśīḥ: his wishes have dripped away (ghṛta), dissolved. acalaḥ: there is no moving from his nature, power, knowledge or other qualities. calaḥ: he moves in the form of the wind.

अमानी मानदो मान्यो लोकस्वामी त्रिलोकधृक् । सुमेधा मेधजो धन्यः सत्यमेधा धराधरः ॥ ९३ ॥
amānī mānado mānyo lokasvāmī trilokadhṛk | sumedhā medhajo dhanyaḥ satyamedhā dharādharaḥ || 93 ||
Padaccheda
अमानी मान-दः मान्यः लोक-स्वामी त्रि-लोक-धृक् सु-मेधाः मेध-जः धन्यः सत्य-मेधाः धरा-धरः
amānī māna-daḥ mānyaḥ loka-svāmī tri-loka-dhṛk su-medhāḥ medha-jaḥ dhanyaḥ satya-medhāḥ dharā-dharaḥ
Word by word
(He is) Amānī, who takes nothing that is not the Self for the Self; Mānada, who gives that notion to all by his māyā (and honour to his devotees, and takes it from the knowers of truth); Mānya, honoured by all, being the lord of all; Lokasvāmī, master of the fourteen worlds; Trilokadhṛk, who holds the three worlds; Sumedhā, of good intelligence; Medhaja, born in sacrifice; Dhanya, who has all he seeks; Satyamedhā, whose intelligence is true; Dharādhara, who holds the whole earth (with his portions, Śeṣa and the rest).

Bhāṣya Amānī: being pure consciousness, he has no notion of self in what is not the Self. mānadaḥ: by his māyā he gives all the notion of self in what is not the Self; or gives honour to his devotees; or cuts off that notion in the knowers of the truth. mānyaḥ: to be honoured and worshipped by all, as the lord of all. lokasvāmī: as lord of the fourteen worlds. trilokadhṛk: he holds the three worlds. sumedhāḥ: his intelligence is good (Pāṇini 5.4.122). medhajaḥ: born in the sacrifice (medha). dhanyaḥ: who has attained his ends. satyamedhāḥ: his intelligence is true. dharādharaḥ: by all his portions, Śeṣa and the others, he holds the whole earth.

तेजोवृषो द्युतिधरः सर्वशस्त्रभृतां वरः । प्रग्रहो निग्रहो व्यग्रो नैकशृङ्गो गदाग्रजः ॥ ९४ ॥
tejovṛṣo dyutidharaḥ sarvaśastrabhṛtāṃ varaḥ | pragraho nigraho vyagro naikaśṛṅgo gadāgrajaḥ || 94 ||
Padaccheda
तेजः-वृषः द्युति-धरः सर्व-शस्त्र-भृताम् वरः प्रग्रहः निग्रहः व्यग्रः नैक-शृङ्गः गद-अग्रजः
tejaḥ-vṛṣaḥ dyuti-dharaḥ sarva-śastra-bhṛtām varaḥ pragrahaḥ nigrahaḥ vyagraḥ naika-śṛṅgaḥ gada-agrajaḥ
Word by word
(He is) Tejovṛṣa, who as the sun always rains the waters; Dyutidhara, who bears the beauty of his limbs; the best of all bearers of weapons; Pragraha, who accepts the leaf and flower offered by devotees (and reins in the horses of the senses); Nigraha, who holds all in check; Vyagra, who has no end (and is eager to give devotees their wish); Naikaśṛṅga, four-horned; Gadāgraja, (manifest first by the mantra, or elder brother of Gada).

Bhāṣya Tejovṛṣaḥ: as the sun he always rains the waters (tejas). dyutidharaḥ: he bears the beauty of his limbs. sarvaśastrabhṛtāṃ varaḥ: best of all who bear weapons. pragrahaḥ: he takes the leaf, flower and the like offered by devotees; or by his grace he binds as with reins the horses of the untamed senses running in the forest of objects (Pāṇini 1.1.15 on the word). nigrahaḥ: by his power he holds all in check. vyagraḥ: his end (agra), destruction, has gone; or he is eager in giving devotees what they wish. naikaśṛṅgaḥ: four-horned (‘four horns’, Ṛgveda 4.58.3). gadāgrajaḥ: he is born first (agra) by the mantra (nigada), the ni dropped; or he was born before Gada, the younger brother of Vāsudeva.

चतुर्मूर्तिश्चतुर्बाहुश्चतुर्व्यूहश्चतुर्गतिः । चतुरात्मा चतुर्भावश्चतुर्वेदविदेकपात् ॥ ९५ ॥
caturmūrtiś caturbāhuś caturvyūhaś caturgatiḥ | caturātmā caturbhāvaś caturvedavid ekapāt || 95 ||
Padaccheda
चतुर्-मूर्तिः चतुर्-बाहुः चतुर्-व्यूहः चतुर्-गतिः चतुर्-आत्मा चतुर्-भावः चतुर्-वेद-वित् एक-पात्
catur-mūrtiḥ catur-bāhuḥ catur-vyūhaḥ catur-gatiḥ catur-ātmā catur-bhāvaḥ catur-veda-vit eka-pāt
Word by word
(He is) Caturmūrti, of four forms (Virāj, the Thread-self, the Unmanifest, the Fourth; or white, red, yellow, dark); Caturbāhu, four-armed (a name proper to Vāsudeva); Caturvyūha, whose four emanations are the four puruṣas; Caturgati, the goal of the four stages and four classes; Caturātmā, whose mind is clear (free of attachment), or the fourfold inner organ; Caturbhāva, from whom the four human ends arise; Caturvedavit, who knows the meaning of the four Vedas; Ekapāt, of one foot (all beings are a quarter of him).

Bhāṣya Caturmūrtiḥ: his four forms are Virāj, the Sūtrātman, the Unmanifest and the Fourth; or white, red, yellow and dark. caturbāhuḥ: four arms are his; the name is fixed for Vāsudeva. caturvyūhaḥ: his four emanations are the four puruṣas of the Ṛgvedic Upaniṣad: the bodily puruṣa, the metrical puruṣa, the Veda-puruṣa and the great puruṣa. caturgatiḥ: the goal of the four stages and four classes who do as enjoined. caturātmā: his mind is clear (catura), free of attachment and the rest; or he is the fourfold inner organ, mind, intellect, ego and memory. caturbhāvaḥ: the four human ends, dharma, wealth, desire and liberation, arise from him. caturvedavit: he knows the meaning of the four Vedas as it is. ekapāt: one foot is his (‘all beings are a quarter of him’, Ṛgveda 10.90.3; ‘I stand supporting this whole world with one portion’, Gītā 10.42).

समावर्तोऽनिवृत्तात्मा दुर्जयो दुरतिक्रमः । दुर्लभो दुर्गमो दुर्गो दुरावासो दुरारिहा ॥ ९६ ॥
samāvarto 'nivṛttātmā durjayo duratikramaḥ | durlabho durgamo durgo durāvāso durārihā || 96 ||
Padaccheda
समावर्तः अनिवृत्त-आत्मा दुर्जयः दुरतिक्रमः दुर्लभः दुर्गमः दुर्गः दुरावासः दुरारि-हा
samāvartaḥ anivṛtta-ātmā durjayaḥ duratikramaḥ durlabhaḥ durgamaḥ durgaḥ durāvāsaḥ durāri-hā
Word by word
(He is) Samāvarta, who rightly turns the wheel of the round; Anivṛttātmā, who, being everywhere, is turned away from nothing; Durjaya, not to be conquered; Duratikrama, whose command none transgresses; Durlabha, gained only by rare devotion; Durgama, known with difficulty; Durga, reached with difficulty (hindered by obstacles); Durāvāsa, lodged in the mind by yogins only with difficulty; Durārihā, slayer of the evil foes, the dānavas.

Bhāṣya Samāvartaḥ: he turns the wheel of the round rightly. anivṛttātmā: being present everywhere, he is not turned away from objects. durjayaḥ: he cannot be conquered. duratikramaḥ: being the cause of fear, the sun and the others do not transgress his command (‘from fear of him fire burns, from fear the sun gives heat, from fear Indra and the wind, and Death runs as the fifth’, Kaṭha 2.3.3; ‘a great fear, a raised thunderbolt’, Kaṭha 2.3.2). durlabhaḥ: gained by rare devotion (‘in men whose sins are spent by austerity, knowledge and absorption over thousands of births, devotion to Kṛṣṇa arises’, Vyāsa; ‘to be gained by undivided devotion’, Gītā 8.22). durgamaḥ: known with difficulty. durgaḥ: reached with difficulty by those hindered by obstacles. durāvāsaḥ: made to dwell in the mind by yogins in absorption only with difficulty. durārihā: he slays the dānavas and other evil foes.

शुभाङ्गो लोकसारङ्गः सुतन्तुस्तन्तुवर्धनः । इन्द्रकर्मा महाकर्मा कृतकर्मा कृतागमः ॥ ९७ ॥
śubhāṅgo lokasāraṅgaḥ sutantus tantuvardhanaḥ | indrakarmā mahākarmā kṛtakarmā kṛtāgamaḥ || 97 ||
Padaccheda
शुभ-अङ्गः लोक-सारङ्गः सु-तन्तुः तन्तु-वर्धनः इन्द्र-कर्मा महा-कर्मा कृत-कर्मा कृत-आगमः
śubha-aṅgaḥ loka-sāraṅgaḥ su-tantuḥ tantu-vardhanaḥ indra-karmā mahā-karmā kṛta-karmā kṛta-āgamaḥ
Word by word
(He is) Śubhāṅga, to be meditated on in fair limbs; Lokasāraṅga, who takes the essence of the worlds as a bee (or is known through Om, the essence of the worlds); Sutantu, whose thread, the manifold, is fair; Tantuvardhana, who extends that thread (or cuts it); Indrakarmā, whose deeds are lordly; Mahākarmā, whose works are the great elements; Kṛtakarmā, who has done all (and has nothing to do); Kṛtāgama, who made the scripture, the Veda.

Bhāṣya Śubhāṅgaḥ: as he is to be meditated on with fair limbs. lokasāraṅgaḥ: like a bee (sāraṅga) he takes the essence of the worlds (‘Prajāpati brooded over the worlds’, Chāndogya 2.23.2); or the essence of the worlds is Om, and he is known through it. sutantuḥ: his thread, the spread-out manifold, is fair. tantuvardhanaḥ: he increases that thread, or cuts it. indrakarmā: his deeds are those of Indra, lordly. mahākarmā: his effects are the great elements, space and the rest. kṛtakarmā: having attained all ends he has nothing left to do; or he has done dharma. kṛtāgamaḥ: he made the scripture, the Veda (‘the breath of this great being’, Bṛhadāraṇyaka 2.4.10).

उद्भवः सुन्दरः सुन्दो रत्ननाभः सुलोचनः । अर्को वाजसनः शृङ्गी जयन्तः सर्वविज्जयी ॥ ९८ ॥
udbhavaḥ sundaraḥ sundo ratnanābhaḥ sulocanaḥ | arko vājasanaḥ śṛṅgī jayantaḥ sarvavijjayī || 98 ||
Padaccheda
उद्भवः सुन्दरः सुन्दः रत्न-नाभः सु-लोचनः अर्कः वाज-सनः शृङ्गी जयन्तः सर्व-वित्-जयी
udbhavaḥ sundaraḥ sundaḥ ratna-nābhaḥ su-locanaḥ arkaḥ vāja-sanaḥ śṛṅgī jayantaḥ sarva-vit-jayī
Word by word
(He is) Udbhava, who takes a high birth by his will (or has no birth, being the cause of all); Sundara, beautiful beyond all; Sunda, the compassionate (whose heart is wet); Ratnanābha, whose navel is beautiful as a jewel; Sulocana, whose eyes (and knowledge) are fair; Arka, worshipful even to Brahmā and the others; Vājasana, who gives food to those who ask; Śṛṅgī, (the Fish with a horn in the waters of dissolution); Jayanta, who conquers the foes; Sarvavijjayī, all-knowing and wont to conquer the foes within and without.

Bhāṣya Udbhavaḥ: by his own will he takes a high birth; or birth has gone from him, being the cause of all. sundaraḥ: having a charm surpassing all. sundaḥ: he moistens (und), is tender-hearted, compassionate. ratnanābhaḥ: his navel is lovely as a jewel. sulocanaḥ: his eyes, or his knowledge, are fair. arkaḥ: worthy of worship even by Brahmā and the others who are most worshipful. vājasanaḥ: he gives food (vāja) to those who ask. śṛṅgī: in the waters of dissolution, the Fish with a horn. jayantaḥ: he conquers foes utterly, or is the cause of victory. sarvavijjayī: one name: all-knowing, and wont to conquer the inner foes, attachment and the rest, and the outer ones hard to conquer, Hiraṇyākṣa and the rest.

सुवर्णबिन्दुरक्षोभ्यः सर्ववागीश्वरेश्वरः । महाह्रदो महागर्तो महाभूतो महानिधिः ॥ ९९ ॥
suvarṇabindur akṣobhyaḥ sarvavāgīśvareśvaraḥ | mahāhrado mahāgarto mahābhūto mahānidhiḥ || 99 ||
Padaccheda
सुवर्ण-बिन्दुः अक्षोभ्यः सर्व-वाक्-ईश्वर-ईश्वरः महा-ह्रदः महा-गर्तः महा-भूतः महा-निधिः
suvarṇa-binduḥ akṣobhyaḥ sarva-vāk-īśvara-īśvaraḥ mahā-hradaḥ mahā-gartaḥ mahā-bhūtaḥ mahā-nidhiḥ
Word by word
(He is) Suvarṇabindu, whose limbs are like gold (or the mantra with fair syllable and bindu); Akṣobhya, unshaken (by attachment, by objects, or by the foes of the gods); Sarvavāgīśvareśvara, lord of all the lords of speech (Brahmā and the others); Mahāhrada, the great lake (where yogins plunge into bliss and rest); Mahāgarta, whose māyā is a great abyss (or the great chariot-warrior); Mahābhūta, unbounded by the three times; Mahānidhi, the great treasure in which all beings are laid.

Bhāṣya Suvarṇabinduḥ: his limbs are like gold (‘to the tips of the nails all golden’, Chāndogya 1.6.6); or he is the mantra in which the syllable and the bindu are fair. Here the eighth hundred names are explained. akṣobhyaḥ: not shaken by attachment, aversion and the rest, by sound and the other objects, or by the foes of the thirty gods. sarvavāgīśvareśvaraḥ: lord even of Brahmā and the other lords of speech. mahāhradaḥ: like a great lake: yogins plunge into his bliss and rest at ease. mahāgartaḥ: his māyā is like a great pit, hard to cross (‘my māyā is hard to cross’, Gītā 7.14); or garta means chariot (Nirukta), so the great chariot-warrior, as he is known in the Bhārata. mahābhūtaḥ: his nature is not bounded by the three times. mahānidhiḥ: all beings are laid in him; great and the treasure.

कुमुदः कुन्दरः कुन्दः पर्जन्यः पावनोऽनिलः । अमृताशोऽमृतवपुः सर्वज्ञः सर्वतोमुखः ॥ १०० ॥
kumudaḥ kundaraḥ kundaḥ parjanyaḥ pāvano 'nilaḥ | amṛtāśo 'mṛtavapuḥ sarvajñaḥ sarvatomukhaḥ || 100 ||
Padaccheda
कुमुदः कुन्दरः कुन्दः पर्जन्यः पावनः अनिलः अमृत-आशः अमृत-वपुः सर्व-ज्ञः सर्वतः-मुखः
kumudaḥ kundaraḥ kundaḥ parjanyaḥ pāvanaḥ anilaḥ amṛta-āśaḥ amṛta-vapuḥ sarva-jñaḥ sarvataḥ-mukhaḥ
Word by word
(He is) Kumuda, who gladdens the earth by taking away its burden; Kundara, who gives pure fruits (or, as the Boar, rent the earth); Kunda, pure as the jasmine (or Paraśurāma, who gave the earth to Kaśyapa); Parjanya, the rain-cloud (stilling the three sorrows, raining all desires); Pāvana, who purifies by remembrance; Anila, whom none prompts (ever awake, and easily reached by devotees); Amṛtāśa, who tastes the nectar of the Self; Amṛtavapu, whose body knows no death; Sarvajña, all-knowing; Sarvatomukha, with faces on every side.

Bhāṣya Kumudaḥ: he gladdens the earth (ku) by lightening its burden. kundaraḥ: he gives (rā) pure fruits like the kunda flower, or takes them (lā), r and l being one; or, as the Boar wishing to slay Hiraṇyākṣa, he rent (dārayāmāsa) the earth. kundaḥ: his limbs fair as the kunda, clear as crystal; or he gave (adāt) the earth (ku) to Kaśyapa (Harivaṃśa, of Paraśurāma at the horse sacrifice); or he cut down (dyati) the lords of the earth (‘he who made the earth bare of kṣatriyas many times and cut the forest of arms of Kārtavīrya, Bhārgava, may that Hari bring me welfare’, Viṣṇudharma). parjanyaḥ: like the cloud he stills the three sorrows, of the body and the rest, or rains all desires. pāvanaḥ: he purifies by mere remembrance.

Bhāṣya Anilaḥ: ila is one who prompts, or one who sleeps; he is neither, being ever awake; or, from the root of hardness, he is not hard, easily reached by devotees. amṛtāśaḥ: he tastes the nectar of the Self; or he gave the churned nectar to the gods and tastes it himself; or his hopes (āśā) are imperishable. amṛtavapuḥ: his body is free of death. sarvajñaḥ: he knows all (Muṇḍaka 1.1.9). sarvatomukhaḥ: (‘with eyes, heads and faces everywhere’, Gītā 13.13).

सुलभः सुव्रतः सिद्धः शत्रुजिच्छत्रुतापनः । न्यग्रोधोदुम्बरोऽश्वत्थश्चाणूरान्ध्रनिषूदनः ॥ १०१ ॥
sulabhaḥ suvrataḥ siddhaḥ śatrujic chatrutāpanaḥ | nyagrodhodumbaro 'śvatthaś cāṇūrāndhraniṣūdanaḥ || 101 ||
Padaccheda
सुलभः सु-व्रतः सिद्धः शत्रु-जित् शत्रु-तापनः न्यग्रोधः उदुम्बरः अश्वत्थः चाणूर-अन्ध्र-निषूदनः
sulabhaḥ su-vrataḥ siddhaḥ śatru-jit śatru-tāpanaḥ nyagrodhaḥ udumbaraḥ aśvatthaḥ cāṇūra-andhra-niṣūdanaḥ
Word by word
(He is) Sulabha, easily gained (by a leaf, flower or fruit offered with devotion); Suvrata, who partakes of what is fair (or who turns from food); Siddha, accomplished without dependence; Śatrujit, who conquers the foes of the gods; Śatrutāpana, who scorches them; Nyagrodha, (who stands above all, or covers all beings by his māyā); Udumbara, (risen from the sky as cause, or nourishing the world as food); Aśvattha, (the tree that will not stand till tomorrow); Cāṇūrāndhraniṣūdana, slayer of the Andhra named Cāṇūra.

Bhāṣya Sulabhaḥ: easily gained by leaves, flowers, fruits and the like offered with devotion alone (‘when leaves, flowers, fruits and water are always at hand, free, why do men not strive for liberation toward the ancient Puruṣa, gained by devotion alone?’, Mahābhārata). suvrataḥ: he partakes (vratayati, bhuṅkte) of what is fair; or he turns from eating. siddhaḥ: his being depends on nothing else. śatrujit: the enemies of the gods are his; he conquers them. śatrutāpanaḥ: he scorches them.

Bhāṣya Nyagrodhaḥ: growing down, he stands above all; or, making all beings low, he covers and holds them by his māyā. udumbaraḥ: risen from the sky (ambara) as the cause; or udumbara is food, and as it he nourishes the world (‘the udumbara is strength, food’); in nyagrodhodumbaraḥ the visarga is dropped and the sandhi is of the Vedic kind. aśvatthaḥ: it will not stand (stha) even tomorrow (śvas) (‘this ancient aśvattha with roots above and branches below’, Kaṭha 2.3.1; Gītā 15.1). cāṇūrāndhraniṣūdanaḥ: he slew the Andhra named Cāṇūra.

Note The text reads nyagrodhodumbaraḥ: nyagrodhaḥ and udumbaraḥ, the visarga dropped, as Śaṅkara notes.

सहस्रार्चिः सप्तजिह्वः सप्तैधाः सप्तवाहनः । अमूर्तिरनघोऽचिन्त्यो भयकृद्भयनाशनः ॥ १०२ ॥
sahasrārciḥ saptajihvaḥ saptaidhāḥ saptavāhanaḥ | amūrtir anagho 'cintyo bhayakṛd bhayanāśanaḥ || 102 ||
Padaccheda
सहस्र-अर्चिः सप्त-जिह्वः सप्त-एधाः सप्त-वाहनः अमूर्तिः अनघः अचिन्त्यः भय-कृत् भय-नाशनः
sahasra-arciḥ sapta-jihvaḥ sapta-edhāḥ sapta-vāhanaḥ amūrtiḥ anaghaḥ acintyaḥ bhaya-kṛt bhaya-nāśanaḥ
Word by word
(He is) Sahasrārci, of a thousand rays; Saptajihva, (fire) with seven tongues; Saptaidhā, of seven flames; Saptavāhana, drawn by seven horses (the sun); Amūrti, without the solid form of the moving and unmoving (or without limbs); Anagha, without sorrow or sin; Acintya, not to be thought (being the witness of the knower and the means of knowing); Bhayakṛt, who brings fear to those on the wrong path (or cuts the fear of devotees); Bhayanāśana, who destroys the fear of those who keep the dharma of class and stage.

Bhāṣya Sahasrārciḥ: his rays are thousands, endless (‘if the light of a thousand suns rose at once in the sky’, Gītā 11.12). saptajihvaḥ: he has seven tongues (‘Kālī, Karālī … these are the seven tongues’, Muṇḍaka 1.2.4). saptaidhāḥ: fire, whose fuels, or lights, are seven (‘seven are your fuels, O Agni, seven your tongues’, a mantra). saptavāhanaḥ: seven horses draw him; or one horse named Sapta (‘one horse named Seven draws him’, Ṛgveda 1.164.2). amūrtiḥ: mūrti is the solid form able to hold, moving and unmoving (‘from them, brooded over, form was born’); he is without it; or without a body with joined limbs.

Bhāṣya Anaghaḥ: no sorrow or sin is his. acintyaḥ: as the witness of the knower and the rest he is beyond all the means of knowledge; being unlike the whole manifold, he cannot be thought of as ‘such’. bhayakṛt: he makes fear for those on the wrong path, or cuts away (kṛt) the fear of devotees. bhayanāśanaḥ: he destroys the fear of those who keep the conduct of their class and stage (‘by the man of the conduct of class and stage the supreme Viṣṇu is worshipped; there is no other way that pleases him’, Parāśara).

अणुर्बृहत्कृशः स्थूलो गुणभृन्निर्गुणो महान् । अधृतः स्वधृतः स्वास्यः प्राग्वंशो वंशवर्धनः ॥ १०३ ॥
aṇur bṛhat kṛśaḥ sthūlo guṇabhṛn nirguṇo mahān | adhṛtaḥ svadhṛtaḥ svāsyaḥ prāgvaṃśo vaṃśavardhanaḥ || 103 ||
Padaccheda
अणुः बृहत् कृशः स्थूलः गुण-भृत् निर्गुणः महान् अधृतः स्व-धृतः स्व-आस्यः प्राग्वंशः वंश-वर्धनः
aṇuḥ bṛhat kṛśaḥ sthūlaḥ guṇa-bhṛt nirguṇaḥ mahān adhṛtaḥ sva-dhṛtaḥ sva-āsyaḥ prāgvaṃśaḥ vaṃśa-vardhanaḥ
Word by word
(He is) Aṇu, most subtle; Bṛhat, vast (Brahman, greater than the great); Kṛśa, thin (not a substance); Sthūla, gross (spoken of so, being the Self of all); Guṇabhṛt, who presides over the guṇas in creation and the rest; Nirguṇa, without guṇas in truth; Mahān, great (beyond all that could bind him); Adhṛta, supported by none; Svadhṛta, supported by himself; Svāsya, whose face is fair (from whose mouth the Veda came forth); Prāgvaṃśa, whose line, the manifold, was first; Vaṃśavardhana, who increases (or cuts) that line.

Bhāṣya Aṇuḥ: of surpassing subtlety (‘this subtle Self is to be known by thought’, Muṇḍaka 3.1.9). bṛhat: Brahman, great and making great (‘greater than the great’, Kaṭha 1.2.20). kṛśaḥ: as the denial of his being a substance (‘not gross’, Bṛhadāraṇyaka 3.8.8). sthūlaḥ: called gross figuratively, being the Self of all. guṇabhṛt: he presides over the work of creation, preservation and dissolution of sattva, rajas and tamas. nirguṇaḥ: in truth having no guṇas (‘alone and without guṇas’, Śvetāśvatara 6.11). mahān: being without sound and the other qualities, most subtle, ever pure and all-pervading, nothing that would limit him can be stated even by reasoning (‘without limbs, soundless, bodiless, touchless, great, pure’, Āpastamba).

Bhāṣya Adhṛtaḥ: he supports even earth and the other supporters, and none supports him. svadhṛtaḥ: by whom then is he held? By himself (‘on what does he rest? On his own greatness’, Chāndogya 7.24.1). svāsyaḥ: his face, red as the inside of a lotus, is most fair; or the great mass of words, the Veda, came forth from his mouth to teach the human ends (Bṛhadāraṇyaka 2.4.10). prāgvaṃśaḥ: others' lines come after; his line, the manifold, was first. vaṃśavardhanaḥ: he increases that line, or cuts it.

भारभृत्कथितो योगी योगीशः सर्वकामदः । आश्रमः श्रमणः क्षामः सुपर्णो वायुवाहनः ॥ १०४ ॥
bhārabhṛt kathito yogī yogīśaḥ sarvakāmadaḥ | āśramaḥ śramaṇaḥ kṣāmaḥ suparṇo vāyuvāhanaḥ || 104 ||
Padaccheda
भार-भृत् कथितः योगी योगि-ईशः सर्व-काम-दः आश्रमः श्रमणः क्षामः सुपर्णः वायु-वाहनः
bhāra-bhṛt kathitaḥ yogī yogi-īśaḥ sarva-kāma-daḥ āśramaḥ śramaṇaḥ kṣāmaḥ suparṇaḥ vāyu-vāhanaḥ
Word by word
(He is) Bhārabhṛt, who as Ananta bears the burden of the earth; Kathita, the one declared (supreme by all the Vedas); Yogī, reached by knowledge (or ever absorbed in himself); Yogīśa, lord of yogins, unhindered; Sarvakāmada, who gives all desires; Āśrama, the resting-place of all who wander in the forest of the round; Śramaṇa, who afflicts the undiscerning; Kṣāma, who makes all creatures waste; Suparṇa, (the tree of the round) whose fair leaves are the Vedas; Vāyuvāhana, from fear of whom the wind carries beings.

Bhāṣya Bhārabhṛt: as Ananta and the rest he bears the burden of the earth. kathitaḥ: he alone is declared supreme by the Vedas and the rest; or declared by all the Vedas (‘the word which all the Vedas declare’, Kaṭha 1.2.15; ‘by all the Vedas I alone am to be known’, Gītā 15.15; ‘in the Veda, the Rāmāyaṇa and the Bhārata Viṣṇu is sung everywhere’); the Kaṭha, having said ‘he reaches the end of the road, the highest abode of Viṣṇu’, declares him as higher than the senses and all the rest, up to ‘beyond Puruṣa there is nothing; that is the end, the highest goal’ (1.3.9–11). yogī: yoga is knowledge, and he is reached by it alone; or yoga is absorption, and he ever holds himself absorbed in himself. yogīśaḥ: other yogins are struck by obstacles and fall from their state; he, free of them, is their lord. sarvakāmadaḥ: he always gives all desires (Brahmasūtra 3.2.38).

Bhāṣya Āśramaḥ: like a hermitage, he is the resting-place of all who wander in the forest of the round. śramaṇaḥ: he torments all the undiscerning. kṣāmaḥ: he makes all creatures waste away. suparṇaḥ: the tree of the round, whose fair leaves are the Vedas (‘whose leaves are the Vedas’, Gītā 15.1). vāyuvāhanaḥ: from fear of him the wind carries beings (‘from fear of him the wind blows’, Taittirīya 2.8).

धनुर्धरो धनुर्वेदो दण्डो दमयिता दमः । अपराजितः सर्वसहो नियन्ता नियमोऽयमः ॥ १०५ ॥
dhanurdharo dhanurvedo daṇḍo damayitā damaḥ | aparājitaḥ sarvasaho niyantā niyamo 'yamaḥ || 105 ||
Padaccheda
धनुः-धरः धनुः-वेदः दण्डः दमयिता दमः अपराजितः सर्व-सहः नियन्ता नियमः अयमः
dhanuḥ-dharaḥ dhanuḥ-vedaḥ daṇḍaḥ damayitā damaḥ aparājitaḥ sarva-sahaḥ niyantā niyamaḥ ayamaḥ
Word by word
(He is) Dhanurdhara, (Rāma) who held the great bow; Dhanurveda, who knows the science of the bow; Daṇḍa, the rod of those who subdue; Damayitā, who subdues creatures (as Yama and as kings); Dama, the restraint (the fruit of the rod); Aparājita, never defeated; Sarvasaha, able in every work (or bearing all foes); Niyantā, who sets all in their places; Aniyama, (having no ruler over him); Ayama, to whom death does not come.

Bhāṣya Dhanurdharaḥ: the glorious Rāma, who held the great bow. dhanurvedaḥ: that same son of Daśaratha knows the science of the bow. daṇḍaḥ: the rod of those who subdue (‘of subduers I am the rod’, Gītā 10.38). damayitā: as Vaivasvata (Yama), kings and the rest he subdues creatures. damaḥ: restraint, the fruit of the rod in those to be subdued, is also he. aparājitaḥ: not defeated by enemies. sarvasahaḥ: able in all works, or he bears all foes. niyantā: he sets all in their own duties.

Bhāṣya Aniyamaḥ: there is no rule over him, since there is no other controller of the controller of all. ayamaḥ: yama, death, is not his. Or yama and niyama are the limbs of yoga; being reached by them, he is niyama and yama.

Note The edition prints niyantā niyamo 'yamaḥ; Śaṅkara reads first aniyamaḥ and ayamaḥ, then niyamaḥ and yamaḥ. The word-by-word follows the print.

सत्त्ववान्सात्त्विकः सत्यः सत्यधर्मपरायणः । अभिप्रायः प्रियार्होऽर्हः प्रियकृत्प्रीतिवर्धनः ॥ १०६ ॥
sattvavān sāttvikaḥ satyaḥ satyadharmaparāyaṇaḥ | abhiprāyaḥ priyārho 'rhaḥ priyakṛt prītivardhanaḥ || 106 ||
Padaccheda
सत्त्ववान् सात्त्विकः सत्यः सत्य-धर्म-परायणः अभिप्रायः प्रिय-अर्हः अर्हः प्रिय-कृत् प्रीति-वर्धनः
sattvavān sāttvikaḥ satyaḥ satya-dharma-parāyaṇaḥ abhiprāyaḥ priya-arhaḥ arhaḥ priya-kṛt prīti-vardhanaḥ
Word by word
(He is) Sattvavān, of courage, valour and the like; Sāttvika, abiding chiefly in sattva; Satya, good among the good; Satyadharmaparāyaṇa, intent on truth (saying things as they are) and on enjoined dharma; Abhiprāya, the aim sought by seekers of the human ends (to whom the world goes at dissolution); Priyārha, worthy of what is dearest; Arha, worthy of worship (with welcome, seat, praise, water and homage); Priyakṛt, who does what is dear to those who worship him; Prītivardhana, who increases their love.

Bhāṣya Sattvavān: his are courage, valour and the like (sattva). sāttvikaḥ: abiding chiefly in the guṇa sattva. satyaḥ: being good among the good. satyadharmaparāyaṇaḥ: fixed on truth, the telling of things as they are, and on dharma known by injunction. abhiprāyaḥ: those who seek the human ends aim at him; or the world goes toward him at dissolution. priyārhaḥ: worthy of what is dear (‘whatever is most loved in the world, and dear in one's house, should be given to one of virtue, wishing it to be imperishable’). arhaḥ: to be worshipped with welcome, seat, praise, offering of water, foot-water, hymns, bowing and the other means of worship. priyakṛt: not only dear, he does what is dear to those who worship him with praise and the rest. prītivardhanaḥ: he increases their love.

विहायसगतिर्ज्योतिः सुरुचिर्हुतभुग्विभुः । रविर्विरोचनः सूर्यः सविता रविलोचनः ॥ १०७ ॥
vihāyasagatir jyotiḥ surucir hutabhug vibhuḥ | ravir virocanaḥ sūryaḥ savitā ravilocanaḥ || 107 ||
Padaccheda
विहायस-गतिः ज्योतिः सु-रुचिः हुत-भुक् विभुः रविः विरोचनः सूर्यः सविता रवि-लोचनः
vihāyasa-gatiḥ jyotiḥ su-ruciḥ huta-bhuk vibhuḥ raviḥ virocanaḥ sūryaḥ savitā ravi-locanaḥ
Word by word
(He is) Vihāyasagati, whose seat is the sky (the step of Viṣṇu, or the sun); Jyoti, light of itself; Suruci, of fair light (or fair will); Hutabhuk, who eats the oblation (offered to any god); Vibhu, everywhere (lord of the three worlds); Ravi, (the sun who takes up the juices); Virocana, shining in many ways; Sūrya, (who brings forth); Savitā, the begetter of the world; Ravilocana, whose eye is the sun.

Bhāṣya Vihāyasagatiḥ: the sky is his seat: the step of Viṣṇu, or the sun. jyotiḥ: he shines of himself (‘Nārāyaṇa, the supreme light’). suruciḥ: his light, or his will, is fair. hutabhuk: he eats the oblation even in rites offered to all the gods; or he protects (bhunakti) it. vibhuḥ: being everywhere; or lord of the three worlds. raviḥ: the sun, who takes up (ā-dā) the juices (rasa). virocanaḥ: he shines variously. sūryaḥ: from sū, to bring forth or impel (Pāṇini 3.1.114). savitā: he begets the whole world (‘from taking up the juices he is called Ravi; from begetting creatures, Savitṛ’, Viṣṇudharmottara). ravilocanaḥ: the sun is his eye (‘fire is his head, the moon and sun his eyes’, Muṇḍaka 2.1.4).

अनन्तो हुतभुग्भोक्ता सुखदो नैकजोऽग्रजः । अनिर्विण्णः सदामर्षी लोकाधिष्ठानमद्भुतः ॥ १०८ ॥
ananto hutabhug bhoktā sukhado naikajo 'grajaḥ | anirviṇṇaḥ sadāmarṣī lokādhiṣṭhānam adbhutaḥ || 108 ||
Padaccheda
अनन्तः हुत-भुक् भोक्ता सुख-दः नैक-जः अग्र-जः अनिर्विण्णः सदा-मर्षी लोक-अधिष्ठानम् अद्भुतः
anantaḥ huta-bhuk bhoktā sukha-daḥ naika-jaḥ agra-jaḥ anirviṇṇaḥ sadā-marṣī loka-adhiṣṭhānam adbhutaḥ
Word by word
(He is) Ananta, endless (eternal, everywhere, or Śeṣa); Hutabhuk, who eats the oblation; Bhoktā, who enjoys Prakṛti (or protects the world); Sukhada, who gives devotees the happiness of liberation (or, read Asukhada, who cuts off unhappiness); Naikaja, born many times to guard dharma; Agraja, born first (Hiraṇyagarbha); Anirviṇṇa, never weary; Sadāmarṣī, ever forbearing toward the good; Lokādhiṣṭhāna, the ground on which the three worlds stand; Adbhuta, the wonder.

Bhāṣya Anantaḥ: being eternal and everywhere, without bounds of place or time; or in the form of Śeṣa. hutabhuk: he eats the oblation. bhoktā: he enjoys Prakṛti, unconscious, the thing to be enjoyed; or protects the world. sukhadaḥ: he gives devotees happiness, liberation; or, read asukhadaḥ (bhoktā asukhadaḥ), he cuts off (do) unhappiness (asukha). naikajaḥ: born again and again to guard dharma. agrajaḥ: born first, Hiraṇyagarbha (‘Hiraṇyagarbha arose first’). anirviṇṇaḥ: having all his desires and no cause for want, he knows no weariness. sadāmarṣī: he always bears with the good. lokādhiṣṭhānam: Brahman, itself without support, on which as ground the three worlds stand. adbhutaḥ: being a wonder (‘he whom many cannot even hear of, whom many hearing do not know; wonderful is the teller, skilled the one who gains him’, Kaṭha 1.2.7; ‘one sees him as a wonder’, Gītā 2.29); or wondrous in nature, power, act and effect.

सनात्सनातनतमः कपिलः कपिरप्ययः । स्वस्तिदः स्वस्तिकृत्स्वस्ति स्वस्तिभुक्स्वस्तिदक्षिणः ॥ १०९ ॥
sanāt sanātanatamaḥ kapilaḥ kapir apyayaḥ | svastidaḥ svastikṛt svasti svastibhuk svastidakṣiṇaḥ || 109 ||
Padaccheda
सनात् सनातन-तमः कपिलः कपिः अप्ययः स्वस्ति-दः स्वस्ति-कृत् स्वस्ति स्वस्ति-भुक् स्वस्ति-दक्षिणः
sanāt sanātana-tamaḥ kapilaḥ kapiḥ apyayaḥ svasti-daḥ svasti-kṛt svasti svasti-bhuk svasti-dakṣiṇaḥ
Word by word
(He is) Sanāt, time (from of old, an imagining of the supreme); Sanātanatama, most ancient of the ancient (Viriñci and the others); Kapila, the tawny (submarine fire); Kapi, the sun who drinks the waters (or the Boar); Apyaya, into whom the worlds dissolve; Svastida, who gives welfare to devotees; Svastikṛt, who makes it; Svasti, welfare itself (his own nature, supreme bliss); Svastibhuk, who enjoys it (or protects the welfare of devotees); Svastidakṣiṇa, quick and able to give welfare.

Bhāṣya Sanāt: an indeclinable meaning ‘long since’; time too is a construction of the supreme (‘the first form of the supreme Brahman is Puruṣa; the manifest and unmanifest are others; and Time another’, Viṣṇu Purāṇa). sanātanatamaḥ: being the cause of all, more ancient than Viriñci and the other ancient ones. kapilaḥ: the colour of the submarine fire is tawny; he is that form. kapiḥ: ka is water; the sun drinks (pā) it with his rays; or the Boar (‘kapi is the Boar and best’). apyayaḥ: at dissolution the worlds go into him. Here the ninth hundred names are explained.

Bhāṣya Svastidaḥ: he gives welfare to devotees. svastikṛt: he makes it. svasti: his own nature, auspicious, supreme bliss. svastibhuk: he enjoys it; or he protects (bhunakti) the welfare of devotees. svastidakṣiṇaḥ: he grows as welfare, or is able to give it; or dakṣiṇa means quick: he alone can give welfare at once, at whose remembrance all accomplishments are gained (‘remembering him, the vessel of all good arises in a man; I go for refuge to Hari, unborn, eternal’; ‘by remembering Kṛṣṇa the cage of the mass of sins breaks a hundredfold, like a mountain struck by the thunderbolt’).

अरौद्रः कुण्डली चक्री विक्रम्यूर्जितशासनः । शब्दातिगः शब्दसहः शिशिरः शर्वरीकरः ॥ ११० ॥
araudraḥ kuṇḍalī cakrī vikramy ūrjitaśāsanaḥ | śabdātigaḥ śabdasahaḥ śiśiraḥ śarvarīkaraḥ || 110 ||
Padaccheda
अरौद्रः कुण्डली चक्री विक्रमी ऊर्जित-शासनः शब्द-अतिगः शब्द-सहः शिशिरः शर्वरी-करः
araudraḥ kuṇḍalī cakrī vikramī ūrjita-śāsanaḥ śabda-atigaḥ śabda-sahaḥ śiśiraḥ śarvarī-karaḥ
Word by word
(He is) Araudra, in whom there is nothing fierce (no passion, anger or harshness); Kuṇḍalī, (Śeṣa, or wearing earrings like the sun's orb, or the makara-shaped Sāṃkhya and Yoga); Cakrī, who holds the discus Sudarśana (the mind) to protect the worlds; Vikramī, whose stride and valour are unlike others'; Ūrjitaśāsana, whose command (śruti and smṛti) is mighty; Śabdātiga, beyond the reach of words; Śabdasaha, whom all the Vedas declare; Śiśira, the cool resting-place of those burnt by the three sorrows; Śarvarīkara, who makes night (the round for the knower, the Self for the bound).

Bhāṣya Araudraḥ: action is fierce, passion is fierce, anger is fierce; having all his desires, he has no passion, aversion or the like, so nothing fierce. kuṇḍalī: in the form of Śeṣa; or wearing earrings like the orb of the sun; or Sāṃkhya and Yoga, makara-shaped, are his earrings. cakrī: for the protection of all the worlds he holds the discus Sudarśana, which is the principle of mind (‘Viṣṇu holds in his hand the mind in the form of the discus, moving, swifter than the wind’, Viṣṇu Purāṇa). vikramī: his stride, or valour, is unlike that of all others. ūrjitaśāsanaḥ: his command, śruti and smṛti, is mighty (‘śruti and smṛti are my commands; he who transgresses them breaks my command and hates me; though my devotee, he is no Vaiṣṇava’, the Lord).

Bhāṣya Śabdātigaḥ: since genus and the other grounds for words are not in him, words cannot speak of him (‘from which words turn back with the mind, not reaching it’, Taittirīya 2.4). śabdasahaḥ: all the Vedas speak of him in their purport (Kaṭha 1.2.15; Gītā 15.15). śiśiraḥ: the resting-place of those burnt by the three sorrows. śarvarīkaraḥ: for those in the round the Self is as night, for the knowers the round is night; he makes both (‘what is night for all beings, in that the self-controlled is awake; where beings are awake, that is night for the seeing sage’, Gītā 2.69).

अक्रूरः पेशलो दक्षो दक्षिणः क्षमिणां वरः । विद्वत्तमो वीतभयः पुण्यश्रवणकीर्तनः ॥ १११ ॥
akrūraḥ peśalo dakṣo dakṣiṇaḥ kṣamiṇāṃ varaḥ | vidvattamo vītabhayaḥ puṇyaśravaṇakīrtanaḥ || 111 ||
Padaccheda
अक्रूरः पेशलः दक्षः दक्षिणः क्षमिणाम् वरः विद्वत्-तमः वीत-भयः पुण्य-श्रवण-कीर्तनः
akrūraḥ peśalaḥ dakṣaḥ dakṣiṇaḥ kṣamiṇām varaḥ vidvat-tamaḥ vīta-bhayaḥ puṇya-śravaṇa-kīrtanaḥ
Word by word
(He is) Akrūra, without cruelty (having no unfulfilled desire, no anger); Peśala, graceful in deed, mind, speech and body; Dakṣa, grown, able and swift; Dakṣiṇa, (able; or who goes and strikes); the best of the patient (and of those who bear burdens, and of the able); Vidvattama, wisest (knowing all, always); Vītabhaya, from whom the fear of the round has gone; Puṇyaśravaṇakīrtana, to hear and sing of whom brings merit.

Bhāṣya Akrūraḥ: cruelty is a quality of mind, an inner burning with rage born of anger; having all his desires, he has no unfulfilled desire and so no anger, and no cruelty. peśalaḥ: being fair in deed, mind, speech and body. dakṣaḥ: grown, able and quick: all three are fixed in the supreme. dakṣiṇaḥ: has the same sense, with no fault of repetition since the word differs; or from dakṣ, ‘to go’ and ‘to harm’. kṣamiṇāṃ varaḥ: best of the patient, the yogins, and of the earth and the others that bear burdens (‘like the earth in patience’, Vālmīki); bearing the whole egg of Brahmā, he is not weighed down, better even than the earth; or the kṣamins are the able, and he, having all powers, is able in all acts. vidvattamaḥ: knowledge unsurpassed, always, of all things, is his, not others'. vītabhayaḥ: the fear belonging to the round, or the round itself, has gone from him, as he is lord of all and ever free. puṇyaśravaṇakīrtanaḥ: to hear and to tell of him brings merit (as the phalaśruti will say: ‘he who hears this daily and he who tells it meets with nothing ill, here or hereafter’, verse 121).

उत्तारणो दुष्कृतिहा पुण्यो दुःस्वप्ननाशनः । वीरहा रक्षणः सन्तो जीवनः पर्यवस्थितः ॥ ११२ ॥
uttāraṇo duṣkṛtihā puṇyo duḥsvapnanāśanaḥ | vīrahā rakṣaṇaḥ santo jīvanaḥ paryavasthitaḥ || 112 ||
Padaccheda
उत्तारणः दुष्कृति-हा पुण्यः दुःस्वप्न-नाशनः वीर-हा रक्षणः सन्तः जीवनः पर्यवस्थितः
uttāraṇaḥ duṣkṛti-hā puṇyaḥ duḥsvapna-nāśanaḥ vīra-hā rakṣaṇaḥ santaḥ jīvanaḥ paryavasthitaḥ
Word by word
(He is) Uttāraṇa, who carries across the ocean of the round; Duṣkṛtihā, who destroys sins (and sinners); Puṇya, who makes merit (or teaches it by śruti and smṛti); Duḥsvapnanāśana, who destroys the bad dreams that foretell ill; Vīrahā, who ends the wanderings (vi-ira) of beings by giving liberation; Rakṣaṇa, who protects the three worlds through sattva; Santa, (the good, as whom he grows learning and humility); Jīvana, who as breath makes all creatures live; Paryavasthita, who stands pervading the universe on every side.

Bhāṣya Uttāraṇaḥ: he carries across from the ocean of the round. duṣkṛtihā: he destroys the misdeeds called sins, or those who do them. puṇyaḥ: he makes merit for all who remember him; or by the words of śruti and smṛti he tells all of merit. duḥsvapnanāśanaḥ: meditated on, praised, named and worshipped, he destroys the bad dreams that foretell coming ill. vīrahā: by giving liberation he ends the various goings of those in the round. rakṣaṇaḥ: presiding over sattva, he protects the three worlds (agent suffix by Pāṇini 3.1.134). santaḥ: those who walk the good path; in their form he grows knowledge and humility. jīvanaḥ: as breath he makes all creatures live. paryavasthitaḥ: he stands pervading the universe on every side.

अनन्तरूपोऽनन्तश्रीर्जितमन्युर्भयापहः । चतुरश्रो गभीरात्मा विदिशो व्यादिशो दिशः ॥ ११३ ॥
anantarūpo 'nantaśrīr jitamanyur bhayāpahaḥ | caturaśro gabhīrātmā vidiśo vyādiśo diśaḥ || 113 ||
Padaccheda
अनन्त-रूपः अनन्त-श्रीः जित-मन्युः भय-अपहः चतुर्-अश्रः गभीर-आत्मा विदिशः व्यादिशः दिशः
ananta-rūpaḥ ananta-śrīḥ jita-manyuḥ bhaya-apahaḥ catur-aśraḥ gabhīra-ātmā vidiśaḥ vyādiśaḥ diśaḥ
Word by word
(He is) Anantarūpa, of endless forms (as the manifold); Anantaśrī, of unmeasured power; Jitamanyu, who has conquered anger; Bhayāpaha, who removes men's fear of the round; Caturaśra, just (giving each the fruit of his deeds); Gabhīrātmā, whose nature and mind none can fathom; Vidiśa, who distributes the various fruits; Vyādiśa, who gives Śakra and the others their various commands; Diśa, who as the Veda points out the fruits of all acts.

Bhāṣya Anantarūpaḥ: as the manifold of the universe his forms are endless. anantaśrīḥ: his power (śrī), the supreme śakti, is unmeasured (‘his supreme power is heard of as manifold’, Śvetāśvatara 6.8). jitamanyuḥ: he has conquered anger. bhayāpahaḥ: he removes men's fear born of the round. caturaśraḥ: just, for he gives men the fruit that fits their acts. gabhīrātmā: his nature, or mind, is deep, not to be measured. vidiśaḥ: he gives those entitled their various fruits. vyādiśaḥ: he gives Śakra and the others their various commands. diśaḥ: as the Veda he points out the fruits of all acts.

अनादिर्भूर्भुवो लक्ष्मीः सुवीरो रुचिराङ्गदः । जननो जनजन्मादिर्भीमो भीमपराक्रमः ॥ ११४ ॥
anādir bhūrbhuvo lakṣmīḥ suvīro rucirāṅgadaḥ | janano janajanmādir bhīmo bhīmaparākramaḥ || 114 ||
Padaccheda
अनादिः भूः-भुवः लक्ष्मीः सु-वीरः रुचिर-अङ्गदः जननः जन-जन्म-आदिः भीमः भीम-पराक्रमः
anādiḥ bhūḥ-bhuvaḥ lakṣmīḥ su-vīraḥ rucira-aṅgadaḥ jananaḥ jana-janma-ādiḥ bhīmaḥ bhīma-parākramaḥ
Word by word
(He is) Anādi, without beginning, being the cause of all; Bhūrbhuva, the support (bhūḥ) of the earth (bhuvaḥ); Lakṣmī, (its) splendour (or the knowledge of the Self); Suvīra, whose goings are fair and many; Rucirāṅgada, with lovely armlets; Janana, who begets creatures; Janajanmādi, the root cause of the birth of creatures; Bhīma, the cause of fear; Bhīmaparākrama, whose valour in his appearances is the cause of fear to the asuras.

Bhāṣya Anādiḥ: no cause (ādi) is his, being the cause of all. bhūrbhuvaḥ: bhūḥ, the support, of bhuvaḥ, the earth, known as the support of all beings: the support even of the earth. Or: not only is he the support of the earth, he is its splendour (lakṣmī), bhuvo lakṣmīḥ; or bhūḥ is this world, bhuvaḥ the world Bhuvar (the mid-region), and lakṣmī the knowledge of the Self (‘you, goddess, are the knowledge of the Self’, Śrīstuti); or he is the splendour of earth and mid-region. suvīraḥ: his goings (īra) are fair and many; or he moves in many fair ways. rucirāṅgadaḥ: his armlets are lovely and auspicious. jananaḥ: he begets creatures. janajanmādiḥ: the root cause of the birth of those who are born. bhīmaḥ: as the cause of fear (Pāṇini 3.4.74; ‘a great fear, a raised thunderbolt’, Kaṭha 2.3.2). bhīmaparākramaḥ: in his appearances his valour is the cause of fear to the asuras and others.

Note The text reads anādir bhūrbhuvo lakṣmīḥ; Śaṅkara divides it in more than one way, as given.

आधारनिलयोऽधाता पुष्पहासः प्रजागरः । ऊर्ध्वगः सत्पथाचारः प्राणदः प्रणवः पणः ॥ ११५ ॥
ādhāranilayo 'dhātā puṣpahāsaḥ prajāgaraḥ | ūrdhvagaḥ satpathācāraḥ prāṇadaḥ praṇavaḥ paṇaḥ || 115 ||
Padaccheda
आधार-निलयः अधाता पुष्प-हासः प्रजागरः ऊर्ध्व-गः सत्-पथ-आचारः प्राण-दः प्रणवः पणः
ādhāra-nilayaḥ adhātā puṣpa-hāsaḥ prajāgaraḥ ūrdhva-gaḥ sat-patha-ācāraḥ prāṇa-daḥ praṇavaḥ paṇaḥ
Word by word
(He is) Ādhāranilaya, the support of the five elements that support all; Adhātā, whom none supports (or who drinks up all creatures at dissolution); Puṣpahāsa, who opens out as the manifold like a bud into a flower; Prajāgara, ever awake; Ūrdhvaga, standing above all; Satpathācāra, who does the deeds of the good; Prāṇada, who gives life to Parīkṣit and others who were dead; Praṇava, (Om, the name of the supreme, one with him); Paṇa, who deals (making names and forms, and giving the fruits of deeds).

Bhāṣya Ādhāranilayaḥ: the support of the five elements, earth and the rest, which are the supports. adhātā: there is no other supporter of him who holds himself; or at dissolution he drinks (dhe) all creatures. puṣpahāsaḥ: as the buds of flowers open out, so he opens out as the manifold. prajāgaraḥ: being ever awake by nature. ūrdhvagaḥ: he stands above all. satpathācāraḥ: the deeds of the good are good paths; he does them. prāṇadaḥ: he made the dead live, Parīkṣit and others. praṇavaḥ: Om, the name of the supreme Self; he is called so by the figure of non-difference. paṇaḥ: paṇ means dealing; he does it (‘the wise one, making all forms and giving them names, goes on speaking’, Taittirīya Āraṇyaka 3.12.7); or, gathering all meritorious acts as a price, he gives their fruit to those entitled.

प्रमाणं प्राणनिलयः प्राणभृत्प्राणजीवनः । तत्त्वं तत्त्वविदेकात्मा जन्ममृत्युजरातिगः ॥ ११६ ॥
pramāṇaṃ prāṇanilayaḥ prāṇabhṛt prāṇajīvanaḥ | tattvaṃ tattvavid ekātmā janmamṛtyujarātigaḥ || 116 ||
Padaccheda
प्रमाणम् प्राण-निलयः प्राण-भृत् प्राण-जीवनः तत्त्वम् तत्त्व-वित् एक-आत्मा जन्म-मृत्यु-जरा-अतिगः
pramāṇam prāṇa-nilayaḥ prāṇa-bhṛt prāṇa-jīvanaḥ tattvam tattva-vit eka-ātmā janma-mṛtyu-jarā-atigaḥ
Word by word
(He is) Pramāṇa, knowing itself, self-luminous consciousness; Prāṇanilaya, in whom the senses and breaths, and the jīvas, come to rest; Prāṇabhṛt, who as food nourishes the breaths; Prāṇajīvana, who makes creatures live by the breaths; Tattva, the truth; Tattvavit, who knows his own nature as it is; Ekātmā, the one Self; Janmamṛtyujarātiga, who has gone beyond birth, death, old age and the other changes.

Bhāṣya Pramāṇam: knowing, consciousness, self-luminous (‘Brahman is consciousness’, Aitareya 3.1.3; ‘in truth knowledge, wholly pure, which through error of sight stands as things’, Viṣṇu Purāṇa). prāṇanilayaḥ: the senses are brought into the jīva, depending on it, or the breaths that hold the body rest in it; or the jīva, which breathes, dissolves in the supreme Puruṣa; or he withdraws breaths and jīvas. prāṇabhṛt: as food he nourishes the breaths. prāṇajīvanaḥ: by the winds called breaths he makes creatures live (‘no mortal lives by prāṇa or apāna; they live by another on whom these two rest’, Kaṭha 2.2.5). tattvam: tattva, tathya, amṛta, satya, paramārtha: words of one sense naming Brahman, the highest reality. tattvavit: he knows his own nature as it is. ekātmā: one and the Self (‘the Self alone was this in the beginning’, Aitareya 1.1.1; ‘because it reaches, takes, eats the objects here, and its being is unbroken, it is called ātman’). janmamṛtyujarātigaḥ: he goes beyond the six changes, ‘is born, exists, grows, changes, wanes, perishes’ (‘the wise one is not born, nor dies’, Kaṭha 1.2.18).

भूर्भुवःस्वस्तरुस्तारः सविता प्रपितामहः । यज्ञो यज्ञपतिर्यज्वा यज्ञाङ्गो यज्ञवाहनः ॥ ११७ ॥
bhūrbhuvaḥsvastarus tāraḥ savitā prapitāmahaḥ | yajño yajñapatir yajvā yajñāṅgo yajñavāhanaḥ || 117 ||
Padaccheda
भूः-भुवः-स्वः-तरुः तारः सविता प्रपितामहः यज्ञः यज्ञ-पतिः यज्वा यज्ञ-अङ्गः यज्ञ-वाहनः
bhūḥ-bhuvaḥ-svaḥ-taruḥ tāraḥ savitā prapitāmahaḥ yajñaḥ yajña-patiḥ yajvā yajña-aṅgaḥ yajña-vāhanaḥ
Word by word
(He is) Bhūrbhuvaḥsvastaru, who by the three utterances bhūḥ, bhuvaḥ, svaḥ, the essence of the three Vedas, carries the three worlds across (or the tree of the three worlds); Tāra, who carries across the ocean of the round (or Om); Savitā, the father of the whole world; Prapitāmaha, the father even of Brahmā the grandfather; Yajña, (who goes with all); Yajñapati, protector and lord of sacrifices; Yajvā, (standing) as the sacrificer; Yajñāṅga, (the Boar) whose limbs are sacrifices; Yajñavāhana, who carries the sacrifices that bring fruit.

Bhāṣya Bhūrbhuvaḥsvastaruḥ: the Ṛgvedins call the three utterances bhūḥ, bhuvaḥ, svaḥ bright, the essence of the three Vedas; by offering with them he carries (tarati) the three worlds across, or makes them flow (‘the oblation duly cast in fire reaches the sun; from the sun comes rain, from rain food, from that creatures’, Manu 3.76); or he is the tree (taru) of the round of the three worlds; or he stands pervading the three worlds like a tree. tāraḥ: he carries across the ocean of the round; or Om. savitā: the father of the whole world. prapitāmahaḥ: the father even of the grandfather, Brahmā. yajñaḥ: who goes with (saṃgantṛ). yajñapatiḥ: protector or lord of sacrifices (‘I am the enjoyer and lord of all sacrifices’, Gītā 9.24). yajvā: standing as the sacrificer. yajñāṅgaḥ: the Boar form, whose limbs are sacrifices (the Harivaṃśa verses: Veda his feet, the post his tusk, the rite his hands, the fire his tongue, the darbha his hair …). yajñavāhanaḥ: he carries the sacrifices that cause the fruit.

यज्ञभृद्यज्ञकृद्यज्ञी यज्ञभुग्यज्ञसाधनः । यज्ञान्तकृद्यज्ञगुह्यमन्नमन्नाद एव च ॥ ११८ ॥
yajñabhṛd yajñakṛd yajñī yajñabhug yajñasādhanaḥ | yajñāntakṛd yajñaguhyam annam annāda eva ca || 118 ||
Padaccheda
यज्ञ-भृत् यज्ञ-कृत् यज्ञी यज्ञ-भुक् यज्ञ-साधनः यज्ञ-अन्त-कृत् यज्ञ-गुह्यम् अन्नम् अन्न-अदः एव च
yajña-bhṛt yajña-kṛt yajñī yajña-bhuk yajña-sādhanaḥ yajña-anta-kṛt yajña-guhyam annam anna-adaḥ eva ca
Word by word
(He is) Yajñabhṛt, who bears or guards the sacrifice; Yajñakṛt, who makes it at the world's beginning (and cuts it at the end); Yajñī, the principal, whom the sacrifices worship; Yajñabhuk, who enjoys or protects it; Yajñasādhana, reached by sacrifices; Yajñāntakṛt, who brings it to its end, the fruit; Yajñaguhya, the secret of sacrifice (the sacrifice of knowledge, Brahman); Anna, food; and Annāda, the eater of food.

Bhāṣya Yajñabhṛt: he bears or protects the sacrifice. yajñakṛt: at the beginning of the world he makes the sacrifice, and at its end cuts it off. yajñī: he is the principal of the sacrifices, which are his worship. yajñabhuk: he enjoys it, or protects it. yajñasādhanaḥ: sacrifices are the means of reaching him. yajñāntakṛt: he makes the end of the sacrifice, the gaining of its fruit; or by the Vaiṣṇava verse and the full oblation he completes it. yajñaguhyam: the secret of sacrifices is the sacrifice of knowledge, or sacrifice without aim at the fruit; by non-difference, Brahman. annam: it is eaten, and it eats beings. annādaḥ: he eats food. eva shows that the whole world, as food and eater, is only enjoyer and enjoyed; ca, that all the names are to be taken together as resting in the one supreme Puruṣa.

आत्मयोनिः स्वयंजातो वैखानः सामगायनः । देवकीनन्दनः स्रष्टा क्षितीशः पापनाशनः ॥ ११९ ॥
ātmayoniḥ svayaṃjāto vaikhānaḥ sāmagāyanaḥ | devakīnandanaḥ sraṣṭā kṣitīśaḥ pāpanāśanaḥ || 119 ||
Padaccheda
आत्म-योनिः स्वयम्-जातः वैखानः साम-गायनः देवकी-नन्दनः स्रष्टा क्षिति-ईशः पाप-नाशनः
ātma-yoniḥ svayam-jātaḥ vaikhānaḥ sāma-gāyanaḥ devakī-nandanaḥ sraṣṭā kṣiti-īśaḥ pāpa-nāśanaḥ
Word by word
(He is) Ātmayoni, himself the material cause and no other; Svayaṃjāta, himself the efficient cause too; Vaikhāna, (the Boar) who dug through the earth to slay Hiraṇyākṣa; Sāmagāyana, who sings the Sāmans; Devakīnandana, the son of Devakī; Sraṣṭā, the creator of all the worlds; Kṣitīśa, Lord of the earth (the son of Daśaratha); Pāpanāśana, who destroys sins when named, worshipped, meditated on and remembered.

Bhāṣya Ātmayoniḥ: he himself is the source, the material cause, and nothing else. svayaṃjātaḥ: to show that he is also the efficient cause; that Hari is both causes is established in ‘Prakṛti too, so as not to contradict the proposition and the example’ (Brahmasūtra 1.4.23). vaikhānaḥ: from digging in a special way; taking the form of the Boar he dug through the earth and slew Hiraṇyākṣa who dwelt in the nether world, as the Purāṇas tell. sāmagāyanaḥ: he sings the Sāmans. devakīnandanaḥ: the son of Devakī (‘the bright lights in the world, the three worlds, the three guardians of the worlds, the three fires and the five oblations, all the gods are the son of Devakī’, Mahābhārata). sraṣṭā: the creator of all the worlds. kṣitīśaḥ: lord of the earth, the son of Daśaratha. pāpanāśanaḥ: named, worshipped, meditated on and remembered, he destroys the mass of sins (‘the sin a man destroys by a fortnight's fast, he destroys by a hundred restraints of breath; what a thousand restraints of breath destroy, the meditation of Hari destroys in a moment’, Vṛddha Śātātapa).

शङ्खभृन्नन्दकी चक्री शार्ङ्गधन्वा गदाधरः । रथाङ्गपाणिरक्षोभ्यः सर्वप्रहरणायुधः । सर्वप्रहरणायुध ॐ नमः ॥ १२० ॥
śaṅkhabhṛn nandakī cakrī śārṅgadhanvā gadādharaḥ | rathāṅgapāṇir akṣobhyaḥ sarvapraharaṇāyudhaḥ | sarvapraharaṇāyudha oṃ namaḥ || 120 ||
Padaccheda
शङ्ख-भृत् नन्दकी चक्री शार्ङ्ग-धन्वा गदा-धरः रथाङ्ग-पाणिः अक्षोभ्यः सर्व-प्रहरण-आयुधः सर्व-प्रहरण-आयुधः ॐ नमः
śaṅkha-bhṛt nandakī cakrī śārṅga-dhanvā gadā-dharaḥ rathāṅga-pāṇiḥ akṣobhyaḥ sarva-praharaṇa-āyudhaḥ sarva-praharaṇa-āyudhaḥ om namaḥ
Word by word
(He is) Śaṅkhabhṛt, who bears the conch Pāñcajanya (the ego from which the elements arise); Nandakī, who bears the sword Nandaka (knowledge); Cakrī, who bears the discus Sudarśana (the mind), or turns the wheel of the round; Śārṅgadhanvā, whose bow is Śārṅga (the senses and the ego); Gadādhara, who bears the mace Kaumodakī (the intellect); Rathāṅgapāṇi, with the wheel in his hand; Akṣobhya, therefore unshakable; Sarvapraharaṇāyudha, whose weapons are all things that strike. To Sarvapraharaṇāyudha: Om, homage.

Bhāṣya Śaṅkhabhṛt: he bears the conch Pāñcajanya, which is the ego from which the elements arise. nandakī: his is the sword Nandaka, made of knowledge. cakrī: his is the discus Sudarśana, the principle of mind; or by his command the wheel of the round turns. śārṅgadhanvā: his bow Śārṅga is the senses and the ego (Pāṇini 5.4.132). gadādharaḥ: he bears the mace Kaumodakī, the principle of intellect. rathāṅgapāṇiḥ: the wheel is in his hand. akṣobhyaḥ: therefore none can shake him. sarvapraharaṇāyudhaḥ: it is not fixed that these alone are his weapons; all things that strike are his weapons, even those not known as weapons, such as the nails: this shows that his resolves are true and that he is lord of all (‘he is the lord of all’, Bṛhadāraṇyaka 4.4.22).

Bhāṣya On the close: the name is said twice to mark the end. Om is for auspiciousness (‘Om and atha, these two came forth of old from Brahmā's throat; so both are auspicious’). Saying ‘homage’ at the end, he worships (‘we offer you the most words of homage’, Īśa 18; ‘that is the right time, that the right star, that the holy day, that the success of the act, where Hari is first bowed to’; ‘first’ stands for the end too, since the wise also bow at the close). The fruit of bowing has been shown before (verse 10): ‘even one bow made to Kṛṣṇa equals the bath at the end of ten horse sacrifices; the one who made ten horse sacrifices is born again, the one who bowed to Kṛṣṇa is not’. Here the tenth hundred names are explained.

Note The edition prints the last name twice, the second time with Om and namaḥ, as a separate line of the verse.

इतीदं कीर्तनीयस्य केशवस्य महात्मनः । नाम्नां सहस्रं दिव्यानामशेषेण प्रकीर्तितम् ॥ १२१ ॥
itīdaṃ kīrtanīyasya keśavasya mahātmanaḥ | nāmnāṃ sahasraṃ divyānām aśeṣeṇa prakīrtitam || 121 ||
Padaccheda
इति इदम् कीर्तनीयस्य केशवस्य महात्मनः नाम्नाम् सहस्रम् दिव्यानाम् अशेषेण प्रकीर्तितम्
iti idam kīrtanīyasya keśavasya mahātmanaḥ nāmnām sahasram divyānām aśeṣeṇa prakīrtitam
Word by word
Thus this thousand of the divine names of the great-souled Keśava, who is worthy of praise, has been proclaimed in full.

Bhāṣya Before the verse: thus the tenth hundred of the names has been explained.

Bhāṣya ‘thus this’ shows that exactly a thousand names, neither fewer nor more, have been told; by saying ‘a thousand of the divine names’, not of ordinary ones, the count is shown to hold in another way too. Since the question at the start used the word japa (‘repeating what is a creature freed?’, verse 3), the word ‘recites’ (kīrtayet, verse 122) also means japa, which is of three kinds: aloud, under the breath and in the mind.

य इदं शृणुयान्नित्यं यश्चापि परिकीर्तयेत् । नाशुभं प्राप्नुयात्किंचित्सोऽमुत्रेह च मानवः ॥ १२२ ॥
ya idaṃ śṛṇuyān nityaṃ yaś cāpi parikīrtayet | nāśubhaṃ prāpnuyāt kiṃcit so 'mutreha ca mānavaḥ || 122 ||
Padaccheda
यः इदम् शृणुयात् नित्यम् यः च अपि परिकीर्तयेत् न अशुभम् प्राप्नुयात् किंचित् सः अमुत्र इह च मानवः
yaḥ idam śṛṇuyāt nityam yaḥ ca api parikīrtayet na aśubham prāpnuyāt kiṃcit saḥ amutra iha ca mānavaḥ
Word by word
The man who hears this daily, and he who recites it, meets with nothing ill, in the other world or here.

Bhāṣya The sense is plain. ‘In the other world’ is said to show that even one who has reached the other world meets no ill, unlike Yayāti, Nahuṣa and others who fell.

वेदान्तगो ब्राह्मणः स्यात्क्षत्रियो विजयी भवेत् । वैश्यो धनसमृद्धः स्याच्छूद्रः सुखमवाप्नुयात् ॥ १२३ ॥
vedāntago brāhmaṇaḥ syāt kṣatriyo vijayī bhavet | vaiśyo dhanasamṛddhaḥ syāc chūdraḥ sukham avāpnuyāt || 123 ||
Padaccheda
वेदान्त-गः ब्राह्मणः स्यात् क्षत्रियः विजयी भवेत् वैश्यः धन-समृद्धः स्यात् शूद्रः सुखम् अवाप्नुयात्
vedānta-gaḥ brāhmaṇaḥ syāt kṣatriyaḥ vijayī bhavet vaiśyaḥ dhana-samṛddhaḥ syāt śūdraḥ sukham avāpnuyāt
Word by word
A brāhmaṇa would come to know the meaning of the Vedānta; a kṣatriya would be victorious; a vaiśya would be rich in wealth; a śūdra would gain happiness (by hearing).

Bhāṣya Vedāntagaḥ: one who reaches, that is, comes to know, the meaning of the Upaniṣads, Brahman. Since the question was ‘repeating what is a creature freed from the bonds of birth and the round?’ (verse 3), one might think that japa as an act frees directly; to show that acts are not the direct cause of liberation, which comes from knowledge alone, it says ‘a brāhmaṇa would come to know the Vedānta’. Acts lead to liberation only through the purifying of the inner organ (‘acts ripen the stains; knowledge is the highest goal; when the stains are ripened by acts, knowledge arises … by acts a creature is bound, by knowledge freed; so the yatis who see the far shore do not act’; ‘knowledge arises in men when sinful action wanes’; ‘brāhmaṇas seek to know him by the study of the Veda, by sacrifice, gift and austerity’, Bṛhadāraṇyaka 4.4.22).

Bhāṣya On the same: liberation is from knowledge alone (‘the knower of Brahman reaches the highest’, Taittirīya 2.1; ‘the knower of the Self crosses grief’, Chāndogya 7.1.3; ‘he who knows Brahman becomes Brahman’, Muṇḍaka 3.2.9; ‘knowing him alone one goes beyond death’, Śvetāśvatara 3.8; ‘not by action, offspring or wealth, by renunciation alone some reached immortality’, Kaivalya 3; ‘the renouncers sure of the meaning of the Vedānta … are all freed’, Muṇḍaka 3.2.6).

Bhāṣya On the śūdra: he would gain happiness by hearing alone, not by the sacrifice of japa (‘the śūdra is not fit for sacrifice’); the Mahābhārata allows hearing (‘one should make the four classes hear, putting a brāhmaṇa first’), and the Harivaṃśa speaks of a good goal for one of śūdra birth by hearing. So ‘a śūdra who hears gains happiness’, the word ‘hears’ being carried from verse 122; for the three higher classes, ‘who recites’.

धर्मार्थी प्राप्नुयाद्धर्ममर्थार्थी चार्थमाप्नुयात् । कामानवाप्नुयात्कामी प्रजार्थी चाप्नुयात्प्रजाम् ॥ १२४ ॥
dharmārthī prāpnuyād dharmam arthārthī cārtham āpnuyāt | kāmān avāpnuyāt kāmī prajārthī cāpnuyāt prajām || 124 ||
Padaccheda
धर्म-अर्थी प्राप्नुयात् धर्मम् अर्थ-अर्थी च अर्थम् आप्नुयात् कामान् अवाप्नुयात् कामी प्रजा-अर्थी च आप्नुयात् प्रजाम्
dharma-arthī prāpnuyāt dharmam artha-arthī ca artham āpnuyāt kāmān avāpnuyāt kāmī prajā-arthī ca āpnuyāt prajām
Word by word
One who seeks dharma would gain dharma, one who seeks wealth would gain wealth; one who desires would gain his desires, and one who seeks offspring would gain offspring.

Bhāṣya Kāma: the going forth of the eye and the other senses, presided over by the mind joined to the self, each to its own object, as it pleases. prajā: offspring, what is born.

Introduction Introducing verses 125–131: in ‘devoted …’ and what follows the special fruit is shown for one specially fit: devoted, pure, ever intent, with one-pointed mind, full of faith.

भक्तिमान्यः सदोत्थाय शुचिस्तद्गतमानसः । सहस्रं वासुदेवस्य नाम्नामेतत्प्रकीर्तयेत् ॥ १२५ ॥
bhaktimān yaḥ sadotthāya śucis tadgatamānasaḥ | sahasraṃ vāsudevasya nāmnām etat prakīrtayet || 125 ||
Padaccheda
भक्तिमान् यः सदा उत्थाय शुचिः तत्-गत-मानसः सहस्रम् वासुदेवस्य नाम्नाम् एतत् प्रकीर्तयेत्
bhaktimān yaḥ sadā utthāya śuciḥ tat-gata-mānasaḥ sahasram vāsudevasya nāmnām etat prakīrtayet
Word by word
He who, devoted, always rising (early), pure, his mind set on him, recites this thousand of the names of Vāsudeva,

Note The edition prints no number with verses 125–130; they are counted on from 124 to 131.

यशः प्राप्नोति विपुलं याति प्राधान्यमेव च । अचलां श्रियमाप्नोति श्रेयः प्राप्नोत्यनुत्तमम् ॥ १२६ ॥
yaśaḥ prāpnoti vipulaṃ yāti prādhānyam eva ca | acalāṃ śriyam āpnoti śreyaḥ prāpnoty anuttamam || 126 ||
Padaccheda
यशः प्राप्नोति विपुलम् याति प्राधान्यम् एव च अचलाम् श्रियम् आप्नोति श्रेयः प्राप्नोति अनुत्तमम्
yaśaḥ prāpnoti vipulam yāti prādhānyam eva ca acalām śriyam āpnoti śreyaḥ prāpnoti anuttamam
Word by word
gains wide fame, comes to eminence, gains unshaken prosperity, and gains the highest good.
न भयं क्वचिदाप्नोति वीर्यं तेजश्च विन्दति । भवत्यरोगो द्युतिमान्बलरूपगुणान्वितः ॥ १२७ ॥
na bhayaṃ kvacid āpnoti vīryaṃ tejaś ca vindati | bhavaty arogo dyutimān balarūpaguṇānvitaḥ || 127 ||
Padaccheda
न भयम् क्वचित् आप्नोति वीर्यम् तेजः च विन्दति भवति अरोगः द्युतिमान् बल-रूप-गुण-अन्वितः
na bhayam kvacit āpnoti vīryam tejaḥ ca vindati bhavati arogaḥ dyutimān bala-rūpa-guṇa-anvitaḥ
Word by word
He meets with fear nowhere; he finds strength and splendour; he becomes free of disease, radiant, endowed with strength, beauty and virtues.
रोगार्तो मुच्यते रोगाद्बद्धो मुच्येत बन्धनात् । भयान्मुच्येत भीतस्तु मुच्येतापन्न आपदः ॥ १२८ ॥
rogārto mucyate rogād baddho mucyeta bandhanāt | bhayān mucyeta bhītas tu mucyetāpanna āpadaḥ || 128 ||
Padaccheda
रोग-आर्तः मुच्यते रोगात् बद्धः मुच्येत बन्धनात् भयात् मुच्येत भीतः तु मुच्येत आपन्नः आपदः
roga-ārtaḥ mucyate rogāt baddhaḥ mucyeta bandhanāt bhayāt mucyeta bhītaḥ tu mucyeta āpannaḥ āpadaḥ
Word by word
One afflicted by disease is freed from disease; one bound is freed from bondage; one afraid is freed from fear; one in distress is freed from distress.
दुर्गाण्यतितरत्याशु पुरुषः पुरुषोत्तमम् । स्तुवन्नामसहस्रेण नित्यं भक्तिसमन्वितः ॥ १२९ ॥
durgāṇy atitaraty āśu puruṣaḥ puruṣottamam | stuvan nāmasahasreṇa nityaṃ bhaktisamanvitaḥ || 129 ||
Padaccheda
दुर्गाणि अतितरति आशु पुरुषः पुरुषोत्तमम् स्तुवन् नाम-सहस्रेण नित्यम् भक्ति-समन्वितः
durgāṇi atitarati āśu puruṣaḥ puruṣottamam stuvan nāma-sahasreṇa nityam bhakti-samanvitaḥ
Word by word
A man who daily, full of devotion, praises Puruṣottama with the thousand names quickly crosses over difficulties.
वासुदेवाश्रयो मर्त्यो वासुदेवपरायणः । सर्वपापविशुद्धात्मा याति ब्रह्म सनातनम् ॥ १३० ॥
vāsudevāśrayo martyo vāsudevaparāyaṇaḥ | sarvapāpaviśuddhātmā yāti brahma sanātanam || 130 ||
Padaccheda
वासुदेव-आश्रयः मर्त्यः वासुदेव-परायणः सर्व-पाप-विशुद्ध-आत्मा याति ब्रह्म सनातनम्
vāsudeva-āśrayaḥ martyaḥ vāsudeva-parāyaṇaḥ sarva-pāpa-viśuddha-ātmā yāti brahma sanātanam
Word by word
The mortal who takes refuge in Vāsudeva, whose goal is Vāsudeva, his self cleansed of all sin, goes to the eternal Brahman.
न वासुदेवभक्तानामशुभं विद्यते क्वचित् । जन्ममृत्युजराव्याधिभयं नैवोपजायते ॥ १३१ ॥
na vāsudevabhaktānām aśubhaṃ vidyate kvacit | janmamṛtyujarāvyādhibhayaṃ naivopajāyate || 131 ||
Padaccheda
न वासुदेव-भक्तानाम् अशुभम् विद्यते क्वचित् जन्म-मृत्यु-जरा-व्याधि-भयम् न एव उपजायते
na vāsudeva-bhaktānām aśubham vidyate kvacit janma-mṛtyu-jarā-vyādhi-bhayam na eva upajāyate
Word by word
For the devotees of Vāsudeva there is no ill anywhere; the fear of birth, death, old age and disease does not arise at all.
इमं स्तवमधीयानः श्रद्धाभक्तिसमन्वितः । युज्येतात्मसुखक्षान्तिश्रीधृतिस्मृतिकीर्तिभिः ॥ १३२ ॥
imaṃ stavam adhīyānaḥ śraddhābhaktisamanvitaḥ | yujyetātmasukhakṣāntiśrīdhṛtismṛtikīrtibhiḥ || 132 ||
Padaccheda
इमम् स्तवम् अधीयानः श्रद्धा-भक्ति-समन्वितः युज्येत आत्म-सुख-क्षान्ति-श्री-धृति-स्मृति-कीर्तिभिः
imam stavam adhīyānaḥ śraddhā-bhakti-samanvitaḥ yujyeta ātma-sukha-kṣānti-śrī-dhṛti-smṛti-kīrtibhiḥ
Word by word
One who recites this hymn, full of faith and devotion, is joined with the happiness of the self, patience, prosperity, firmness, memory and fame.

Bhāṣya Śraddhā: the conviction that it is so; bhakti: devotion, being wholly intent. ātmasukha: the happiness of the self; he is joined with that and with patience and the rest.

न क्रोधो न च मात्सर्यं न लोभो नाशुभा मतिः । भवन्ति कृतपुण्यानां भक्तानां पुरुषोत्तमे ॥ १३३ ॥
na krodho na ca mātsaryaṃ na lobho nāśubhā matiḥ | bhavanti kṛtapuṇyānāṃ bhaktānāṃ puruṣottame || 133 ||
Padaccheda
न क्रोधः न च मात्सर्यम् न लोभः न अशुभा मतिः भवन्ति कृत-पुण्यानाम् भक्तानाम् पुरुषोत्तमे
na krodhaḥ na ca mātsaryam na lobhaḥ na aśubhā matiḥ bhavanti kṛta-puṇyānām bhaktānām puruṣottame
Word by word
Neither anger, nor envy, nor greed, nor evil thought arises in those who have done merit, devoted to Puruṣottama.

Bhāṣya Nakrodhaḥ, nalobhaḥ, nāśubhā matiḥ: the three are compounds with na, the particle without the anubandha (so na does not become a-): anger and the rest do not arise, nor envy. (The print sets नक्रोधो and नलोभो as one word; the word list gives them apart.)

द्यौः सचन्द्रार्कनक्षत्रा खं दिशो भूर्महोदधिः । वासुदेवस्य वीर्येण विधृतानि महात्मनः ॥ १३४ ॥
dyauḥ sacandrārkanakṣatrā khaṃ diśo bhūr mahodadhiḥ | vāsudevasya vīryeṇa vidhṛtāni mahātmanaḥ || 134 ||
Padaccheda
द्यौः स-चन्द्र-अर्क-नक्षत्रा खम् दिशः भूः महा-उदधिः वासुदेवस्य वीर्येण विधृतानि महात्मनः
dyauḥ sa-candra-arka-nakṣatrā kham diśaḥ bhūḥ mahā-udadhiḥ vāsudevasya vīryeṇa vidhṛtāni mahātmanaḥ
Word by word
Heaven with the moon, the sun and the stars, the sky, the quarters, the earth and the great ocean are held by the power of Vāsudeva, the great Self.

Bhāṣya On verses 134–140: by telling the greatness of Vāsudeva, the one to be praised, in ‘heaven with the moon, sun and stars’ and what follows, it shows that the promise of the fruits stated is a statement of fact, not mere praise. And by the sentence within it, ‘of all the traditions conduct is first’ (verse 137), it shows that only one of good conduct is entitled to all the dharmas.

ससुरासुरगन्धर्वं सयक्षोरगराक्षसम् । जगद्वशे वर्ततेदं कृष्णस्य सचराचरम् ॥ १३५ ॥
sasurāsuragandharvaṃ sayakṣoragarākṣasam | jagad vaśe vartatedaṃ kṛṣṇasya sacarācaram || 135 ||
Padaccheda
स-सुर-असुर-गन्धर्वम् स-यक्ष-उरग-राक्षसम् जगत् वशे वर्तते इदम् कृष्णस्य स-चर-अचरम्
sa-sura-asura-gandharvam sa-yakṣa-uraga-rākṣasam jagat vaśe vartate idam kṛṣṇasya sa-cara-acaram
Word by word
This world, moving and unmoving, with gods, asuras and gandharvas, with yakṣas, serpents and rākṣasas, stands under the control of Kṛṣṇa.
इन्द्रियाणि मनो बुद्धिः सत्त्वं तेजो बलं धृतिः । वासुदेवात्मकान्याहुः क्षेत्रं क्षेत्रज्ञ एव च ॥ १३६ ॥
indriyāṇi mano buddhiḥ sattvaṃ tejo balaṃ dhṛtiḥ | vāsudevātmakāny āhuḥ kṣetraṃ kṣetrajña eva ca || 136 ||
Padaccheda
इन्द्रियाणि मनः बुद्धिः सत्त्वम् तेजः बलम् धृतिः वासुदेव-आत्मकानि आहुः क्षेत्रम् क्षेत्र-ज्ञः एव च
indriyāṇi manaḥ buddhiḥ sattvam tejaḥ balam dhṛtiḥ vāsudeva-ātmakāni āhuḥ kṣetram kṣetra-jñaḥ eva ca
Word by word
The senses, mind, intellect, sattva, splendour, strength, firmness: they say these have Vāsudeva for their self, and the field and the knower of the field as well.

Note The edition prints no number with this verse; it is the 136th.

सर्वागमानामाचारः प्रथमं परिकल्पते । आचारप्रभवो धर्मो धर्मस्य प्रभुरच्युतः ॥ १३७ ॥
sarvāgamānām ācāraḥ prathamaṃ parikalpate | ācāraprabhavo dharmo dharmasya prabhur acyutaḥ || 137 ||
Padaccheda
सर्व-आगमानाम् आचारः प्रथमम् परिकल्पते आचार-प्रभवः धर्मः धर्मस्य प्रभुः अच्युतः
sarva-āgamānām ācāraḥ prathamam parikalpate ācāra-prabhavaḥ dharmaḥ dharmasya prabhuḥ acyutaḥ
Word by word
Of all the traditions conduct is held to be first; dharma springs from conduct; the lord of dharma is Acyuta.
ऋषयः पितरो देवा महाभूतानि धातवः । जङ्गमाजङ्गमं चेदं जगन्नारायणोद्भवम् ॥ १३८ ॥
ṛṣayaḥ pitaro devā mahābhūtāni dhātavaḥ | jaṅgamājaṅgamaṃ cedaṃ jagan nārāyaṇodbhavam || 138 ||
Padaccheda
ऋषयः पितरः देवाः महा-भूतानि धातवः जङ्गम-अजङ्गमम् च इदम् जगत् नारायण-उद्भवम्
ṛṣayaḥ pitaraḥ devāḥ mahā-bhūtāni dhātavaḥ jaṅgama-ajaṅgamam ca idam jagat nārāyaṇa-udbhavam
Word by word
The seers, the fathers, the gods, the great elements, the constituents (of the body), and this world, moving and unmoving, have sprung from Nārāyaṇa.
योगो ज्ञानं तथा सांख्यं विद्याः शिल्पादि कर्म च । वेदाः शास्त्राणि विज्ञानमेतत्सर्वं जनार्दनात् ॥ १३९ ॥
yogo jñānaṃ tathā sāṃkhyaṃ vidyāḥ śilpādi karma ca | vedāḥ śāstrāṇi vijñānam etat sarvaṃ janārdanāt || 139 ||
Padaccheda
योगः ज्ञानम् तथा सांख्यम् विद्याः शिल्प-आदि कर्म च वेदाः शास्त्राणि विज्ञानम् एतत् सर्वम् जनार्दनात्
yogaḥ jñānam tathā sāṃkhyam vidyāḥ śilpa-ādi karma ca vedāḥ śāstrāṇi vijñānam etat sarvam janārdanāt
Word by word
Yoga, knowledge and Sāṃkhya, the sciences, crafts and other work, the Vedas, the śāstras, understanding: all this is from Janārdana.
एको विष्णुर्महद्भूतं पृथग्भूतान्यनेकशः । त्रींल्लोकान्व्याप्य भूतात्मा भुङ्क्ते विश्वभुगव्ययः ॥ १४० ॥
eko viṣṇur mahad bhūtaṃ pṛthag bhūtāny anekaśaḥ | trīṃl lokān vyāpya bhūtātmā bhuṅkte viśvabhug avyayaḥ || 140 ||
Padaccheda
एकः विष्णुः महत् भूतम् पृथक् भूतानि अनेकशः त्रीन् लोकान् व्याप्य भूत-आत्मा भुङ्क्ते विश्व-भुक् अव्ययः
ekaḥ viṣṇuḥ mahat bhūtam pṛthak bhūtāni anekaśaḥ trīn lokān vyāpya bhūta-ātmā bhuṅkte viśva-bhuk avyayaḥ
Word by word
Viṣṇu, the one great being, (stands) separately as the many beings; pervading the three worlds, the self of beings, the enjoyer of all, undecaying, enjoys (them).
इमं स्तवं भगवतो विष्णोर्व्यासेन कीर्तितम् । पठेद्य इच्छेत्पुरुषः श्रेयः प्राप्तुं सुखानि च ॥ १४१ ॥
imaṃ stavaṃ bhagavato viṣṇor vyāsena kīrtitam | paṭhed ya icchet puruṣaḥ śreyaḥ prāptuṃ sukhāni ca || 141 ||
Padaccheda
इमम् स्तवम् भगवतः विष्णोः व्यासेन कीर्तितम् पठेत् यः इच्छेत् पुरुषः श्रेयः प्राप्तुम् सुखानि च
imam stavam bhagavataḥ viṣṇoḥ vyāsena kīrtitam paṭhet yaḥ icchet puruṣaḥ śreyaḥ prāptum sukhāni ca
Word by word
The man who would gain the good and happiness should recite this hymn of the Lord Viṣṇu, proclaimed by Vyāsa.

Bhāṣya In ‘this hymn …’ it shows that, since it was made by the Lord Kṛṣṇa Dvaipāyana, who knew the thousand branches, all-knowing, Nārāyaṇa himself, it should be recited with care by all who seek, for the gaining of every fruit.

विश्वेश्वरमजं देवं जगतः प्रभवाप्ययम् । भजन्ति ये पुष्कराक्षं न ते यान्ति पराभवम् ॥ १४२ ॥
viśveśvaram ajaṃ devaṃ jagataḥ prabhavāpyayam | bhajanti ye puṣkarākṣaṃ na te yānti parābhavam || 142 ||
Padaccheda
विश्व-ईश्वरम् अजम् देवम् जगतः प्रभव-अप्ययम् भजन्ति ये पुष्कर-अक्षम् न ते यान्ति पराभवम्
viśva-īśvaram ajam devam jagataḥ prabhava-apyayam bhajanti ye puṣkara-akṣam na te yānti parābhavam
Word by word
Those who worship the lotus-eyed, the lord of all, the unborn god, the origin and end of the world, do not come to defeat.

Note The edition closes the chapter: ‘thus in the glorious Mahābhārata, in the Anuśāsanaparvan, in the dialogue of Bhīṣma and Yudhiṣṭhira, the hymn of the divine thousand names of Viṣṇu, the one hundred and forty-ninth chapter’.

Bhāṣya By ‘the lord of all …’ it shows that by the very worship of the Lord of all those who praise him are blessed, their ends attained, their work done. And Vyāsa says: ‘whatever falls short in the rites of those who act from heedlessness becomes whole by the mere remembrance of Viṣṇu, so says śruti; if one praised the maker of all with the care with which one praises the rich man for wealth, who would not be freed from bondage?’ He closes: ‘this explanation of the thousand names, bringing every happiness, rooted in śruti, smṛti and reasoning, is laid at the feet of Hari’.

Sanskrit text: The Works of Sri Sankaracharya, vol. 13, Sri Vani Vilas Press, Srirangam, 1910, where the Viṣṇusahasranāmastotra (Mahābhārata, Anuśāsanaparvan, in the vulgate text) is printed with Śaṅkara's bhāṣya (pp. 1–170). The edition is in the public domain; Vedic Space typed the stotra from the Internet Archive scan (The Free Indological Collection), without collation against another edition; the edition's readings and its numbering are kept.

Commentary followed: Śaṅkara's Viṣṇusahasranāma-bhāṣya, printed with the stotra in the same volume of the 1910 edition. The English is Vedic Space's own.
